at the quantum level.
Nevertheless, in a
D
-dimensional space-time, one possible extension is given by

O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}
with
r=1/(D-1)
, which reduces to the
T\bar{T}
operator in two dimensions.

This operator has received some attention recently since it is motivated by a particular holographic picture in
D>2

[
Taylor:2018xcy
,
Hartman:2018tkw
]
.
We will not enter into a detailed discussion of the physical properties enjoyed by
O_{T^{2}}^{[1/(D-1)]}
, but simply comment that this combination is invariant under a set of improvement transformations of the stress-energy tensor.
Indeed it is easy to show that such a
T^{2}
operator transforms by,

O_{T^{2}}^{[1/(D-1)]}\rightarrow O_{T^{2}}^{[1/(D-1)]}+\text{total derivatives}~,

(2)

if the (symmetric) stress-energy tensor shifts by the following improvement transformation,

T_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow T_{\mu\nu}+\big(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\big)u~,

(3)

for an arbitrary scalar field
u
.

In four dimensions, there is another choice of interest, specifically
r=1/2
.
In fact, it was shown in
[
Conti:2018jho
]
that the bosonic Born-Infeld action can be obtained by deforming the free Maxwell theory
with the operator
O_{T^{2}}^{[1/2]}
.
2
2

2

It is worth mentioning that another type of higher-dimensional generalization
of
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-deformations, specifically the operator
|\det T|^{1/(D-1)}
, was studied in
[
Cardy:2018sdv
,
Bonelli:2018kik
]
.

In this work, we are going to use
O_{T^{2}}^{[1/2]}
as our deforming operator. Once generalized to the
supersymmetric case, we will see that this operator plays a central role for various models possessing non-linearly realized symmetries.

One interesting property enjoyed by
O_{T^{2}}^{[1/2]}
is its invariance under a shift of the Lagrangian density of the theory, or equivalently a shift of the zero point energy. This can serve as motivation for this particular combination. Under a constant shift of the Lagrangian
density
{\mathcal{L}}
, and correspondingly its stress-energy tensor,

{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}+c~,\qquad T^{\mu\nu}\rightarrow T^{\mu\nu}-c\,\eta^{\mu\nu}~,

(4)

the composite operator
O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}
transforms in the following way:

O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}\rightarrow O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}+2c(2r-1)\Theta+4c^{2}(1-r)~.

(5)

When the theory is not conformal, which is the general situation at an arbitrary point in the flow since the deformation introduces a scale,
and
r\neq 1/2
, the operator
O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}
always transforms in a non-trivial way because of the extra trace term.
This implies that under a constant shift in the Lagrangian, the dynamics is modified which is certainly peculiar since the shift is trivial in the undeformed theory.
3
3

3

It is worth noting that
{T\overline{T}}
in
D=2
shares this peculiarity.

However if
r=\frac{1}{2}
,
O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}
is unaffected up to an honest field-independent cosmological constant term. The shift of the
vacuum energy does not affect the dynamics of the theory, as long as the theory is not coupled to gravity. This property is especially interesting, since the
D=4

{\cal N}=1
Goldstino action, which is also given by a
{T\overline{T}}
flow,
is the low-energy description of supersymmetry breaking which can generate a cosmological
constant.
For these reasons, we will study the particular operator quadratic in stress-energy tensors given by

O_{T^{2}}\equiv T^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{2}~,

(6)

in the remainder of this chapter. More general
T^{2}
type deformations of gauge theories in any spacetime dimension will be considered in Chapter
6
.

2
{\cal N}=1
supercurrent-squared operator in
D=4

We would like to find the
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric extension of the
O_{T^{2}}
operator in four dimensions.
As reviewed in section
1
,
in two dimensions the manifestly supersymmetric
T\bar{T}
deformation is roughly given by the square of the supercurrent
superfields.
One might suspect that a similar construction holds in four dimensions.

For the remainder of this work, we will assume that the
D=4
,
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric theories under our consideration admit
a Ferrara-Zumino (FZ) multiplet of currents
[
Ferrara:1974pz
]
. Generalizations of this case involving
the supercurrent multiplets described in

[
Komargodski:2010rb
,
Dumitrescu:2011iu
,
Gates:1981yc
,
Ambrosetti:2016ieg
,
Gates:1983nr
,
Dienes:2009td
,
Kuzenko:2010am
]
might be possible, but merit separate investigation.
The operator content of the FZ multiplet, which has
12+12
component fields,
includes the conserved supersymmetry current
S_{\mu\alpha}
, its conjugate
\bar{S}_{\mu}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}

and the conserved symmetric energy-momentum tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
:

T_{\mu\nu}=T_{\nu\mu}~,\qquad\partial^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0~,\qquad\partial^{\mu}S_{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}\bar{S}_{\mu}=0~.

(7)

The FZ multiplet also includes a complex scalar field
{\sf x}
, as well as
the
R
-current vector field
j_{\mu}
, which is not necessarily conserved

[
Ferrara:1974pz
]
.

In
D=4
,
{\cal N}=1
superspace,
the FZ multiplet is described by a vector superfield
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mu}
and a complex scalar scalar superfield
\mathcal{X}

satisfying the following constraints:

\bar{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=D_{\alpha}\mathcal{X}~,\qquad\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{X}=0~.

(8)

The constraints can be solved, and the FZ supercurrents expressed in terms of its
12+12

independent components read
4
4

4

For convenience, we have rescaled the supersymmetry current compared to

[
Dumitrescu:2011iu
]
:
S_{\mu}^{\text{here}}=-iS_{\mu}^{\text{there}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j_{\mu}+\theta\Big(S_{\mu}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{\mu}\bar{\chi}\Big)+\bar{\theta}\Big(\bar{S}_{\mu}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\chi\Big)+\frac{i}{2}\theta^{2}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\sf x}-\frac{i}{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\partial_{\mu}{\sf x}

(9)

\displaystyle+\theta\sigma^{\nu}\bar{\theta}\Big(2T_{\mu\nu}-\frac{2}{3}\eta_{\mu\nu}\Theta-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho\sigma}\partial^{\rho}j^{\sigma}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}\Big(\bar{\not{\partial}}S_{\mu}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\not{\partial}\bar{\chi}\Big)-\frac{i}{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\Big({\not{\partial}}{\bar{S}}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{\mu}\bar{\not{\partial}}\chi\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\Big(\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}j_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}j_{\mu}\Big)~,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf x}(y)+\sqrt{2}\theta\chi(y)+\theta^{2}{\sf F}(y)~,~~~

(10a)

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}(\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{S}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\mu}~,\qquad{\sf F}=\frac{2}{3}\Theta+i\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}~,

(10b)

where the chiral coordinate is defined by
y^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}
,
and we used
\not{\partial}=\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu},\,\bar{\not{\partial}}=\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
.

If we seek a manifestly supersymmetric completion of the operator (
6
)
by using combinations of the supercurrent superfields with dimension
4
,
it is clear that the only possibility is the full superspace
integral of a linear combination of
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}
.
Up to total derivatives and terms that vanish by using the supercurrent conservation equations, or equivalently that vanish
on-shell, the
D
-terms of
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}
are given by
5
5

5

The composite
A
(and analogously its conjugate
\bar{A}
) is given by

\displaystyle A=\Big(S_{\mu}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{\mu}\bar{\chi}\Big)\Big({\not{\partial}}{\bar{S}}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\not{\partial}}\chi\Big)=S_{\mu}{\not{\partial}}{\bar{S}}^{\mu}-\bar{\chi}\bar{\not{\partial}}\chi+\sqrt{2}{\bar{S}}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\chi}+\text{total derivatives}~.

(11)

The equality can be obtained with some algebra. Note that the last term drops
after integration by parts because of the conservation equation for
S_{\mu}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{2}|_{\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}}\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}{\mathcal{J}}_{\mu}{\mathcal{J}}_{\nu}|_{\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big(2T_{\mu\nu}-\frac{2}{3}\eta_{\mu\nu}\Theta-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho\sigma}\partial^{\rho}j^{\sigma}\Big)^{2}+j^{\mu}\Big(\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}j_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}j_{\mu}\Big)

(12b)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}{\sf x}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\sf{x}}+\frac{i}{2}\Big(A-\bar{A}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(T_{\mu\nu})^{2}+\frac{4}{9}\Theta^{2}-\frac{5}{4}\Big(\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}\Big)^{2}-\frac{3}{4}j_{\mu}\partial^{2}j^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}{\sf\bar{x}}\partial^{\mu}{\sf x}

\displaystyle+i\Big(S_{\mu}{\not{\partial}}{\bar{S}}^{\mu}-\bar{\chi}\bar{\not{\partial}}\chi\Big)+\text{total derivatives}+{\rm EOM}~,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}|_{\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf F}\bar{\sf F}-\partial_{\mu}{\sf x}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\sf x}-i\bar{\chi}{\bar{\not{\partial}}}\chi+\text{total derivatives}

(13a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{9}\Theta^{2}+(\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu})^{2}-\partial_{\mu}{\sf x}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\sf x}-i\bar{\chi}{\bar{\not{\partial}}}\chi+\text{total derivatives}~.

(13b)

To get a manifestly supersymmetric extension of
O_{T^{2}}=T^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{2}
, we have to consider the following linear
combination

\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\mu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}+\frac{5}{4}\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}\Big)=\frac{1}{16}\mathcal{J}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}-\frac{5}{8}\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}~.

(14)

In fact, the supersymmetric descendant of the supercurrent-squared operator
\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}
is

\displaystyle{\sf O}_{T^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}\theta\,\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}

(15b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{2}+\frac{3}{8}j_{\mu}\partial^{2}j^{\mu}+\frac{3}{8}\partial_{\mu}{\sf x}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\sf x}-\frac{i}{2}\Big(S_{\mu}{\not{\partial}}{\bar{S}}^{\mu}-\frac{9}{4}\bar{\chi}\bar{\not{\partial}}\chi\Big)

\displaystyle+\text{total derivatives}+{\rm EOM}~.

This result shows that
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}
is the natural supersymmetric extension of
O_{T^{2}}
.
However, it is worth emphasizing that in the
D=4
case, the supersymmetric descendent
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{2}}

has extra non-trivial contributions from other currents. This should be contrasted with the
D=2
case where
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}=O_{T^{2}}

up to
{\rm EOM}
and total derivatives, see eq. (
1
).

It actually does not seem possible to find a linear combination of
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}
such that an analogue
of eq. (
1
) holds in
D=4
.
This suggests that, in contrast with the
D=2
case, deformations of a Lagrangian triggered
by the operators
O_{T^{2}}
and
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}

will in general lead to different flows: one manifestly supersymmetric, while the other not.

3
Bosonic Born-Infeld as a
T^{2}
Flow

It was shown in
[
Conti:2018jho
]
that the
D=4
Born-Infeld action arises from a
D>2
generalization of the
T\bar{T}
deformation. Specifically, the operator driving the flow equation was shown to be the
O_{T^{2}}
defined in eq. (
6
) of the preceding section.
In this section we review this result in detail as it is a primary inspiration for our supersymmetric extensions.

The
D=4
bosonic BI action on a flat background
is given by

\displaystyle S_{\text{BI }}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int d^{4}x\;\Big[1-\sqrt{-\det(\eta_{\mu\nu}+\alpha F_{\mu\nu} )}\Big]~,

(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int d^{4}x\;\Big[1-\sqrt{1+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}F^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{4}}{16}(F\tilde{F})^{2}}\Big]~,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int d^{4}x\;F^{2}+{\rm higher~derivative~terms}~,

where
F_{\mu\nu}=(\partial_{\mu}v_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}v_{\mu})
is the field strength for an Abelian gauge field
v_{\mu}
, and

F^{2}\equiv F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu},\qquad F\tilde{F}\equiv F_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\rho\sigma}~.

(2)

The Hilbert stress-energy tensor
for the BI action can be computed straightforwardly and it reads
[
Rasheed:1997ns
]

T^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{F^{\mu\lambda}F^{\nu}{}_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\Big(\sqrt{1+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}F^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{4}}{16}(F\tilde{F})^{2}}-1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}F^{2}\Big)\eta^{\mu\nu}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}F^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{4}}{16}(F\tilde{F})^{2}}}~.

(3)

This can be written in the following useful form

T^{\mu\nu}=\frac{T^{\mu\nu}_{\text{Maxwell}}}{\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}+\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}\frac{A^{2}-B^{2}}{1+A+\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}~,

(4)

where we used the stress-energy tensor for the Maxwell theory

T^{\mu\nu}_{\text{Maxwell}}=-F^{\mu\lambda}F^{\nu}{}_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{4}F^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}~,

(5)

while
A
and
B
are defined by

A=\frac{1}{4}\alpha^{2}F^{2}~,\qquad B=\frac{i}{4}\alpha^{2}F\tilde{F}~.

(6)

It is easy to compute the trace of the stress-energy tensor

\Theta=T^{\mu\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}\frac{A^{2}-B^{2}}{1+A+\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}~,

(7)

where, interestingly, the combination
(A^{2}-B^{2})
proves to be related to the square of
T^{\mu\nu}_{\text{Maxwell}}
. Using the identity

(F\tilde{F})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\rho\sigma})^{2}=4F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\rho}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\sigma\mu}-2(F^{2})^{2}~,

(8)

we see that

T^{2}_{\text{Maxwell}}=F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\rho}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\sigma\mu}-\frac{1}{4}(F^{2})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\Big((F^{2})^{2}+(F\tilde{F})^{2}\Big)=\frac{4}{\alpha^{4}}(A^{2}-B^{2})~.

(9)

Using tracelessness of the free Maxwell stress-energy tensor,
the
O_{T^{2}}
operator can be easily computed:

\displaystyle O_{T^{2}}=T^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(A^{2}-B^{2})}{\alpha^{4}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{A^{2}-B^{2}}{(1+A+\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}})^{2}}\Big)~,~~~~~~~~~

(10a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(A^{2}-B^{2})}{\alpha^{4}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{1+A-\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}{1+A+\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}\Big)~,

(10b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8(A^{2}-B^{2})}{\alpha^{4}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}\frac{1}{1+A+\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}~,

(10c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8(1+A-\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2})}}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+2A+B^{2}}}~.

(10d)

The variation of the BI Lagrangian with respect to the
parameter
\alpha^{2}
can be readily computed,
and it is given by

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}}{\partial\alpha^{2}}=\frac{1+\frac{1}{4}\alpha^{2}F^{2}-\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}F^{2}-\frac{1}{16}\alpha^{4}(F\tilde{F})^{2}}}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{4}F^{2}-\frac{1}{16}\alpha^{4}(F\tilde{F})^{2}}}~.

(11)

Once we use (
6
) it is clear that (
10a
) and (
11
) have exactly the same structure and satisfy

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}}{\partial\alpha^{2}}=\frac{1}{8}O_{T^{2}}~,

(12)

showing that the BI Lagrangian satisfies a
T^{2}
-flow driven by the operator
O_{T^{2}}
.

Before turning to
D=4
supersymmetric analysis, it is worth mentioning that the structure
of the computation relating the
O_{T^{2}}
operator to the bosonic BI theory, which we just reviewed,
is quite similar to what we saw in section
1
for the
D=2

{\cal N}=(2,2)
supersymmetric
T\bar{T}
flows.
For example, in the deformation of the free twisted-chiral multiplet action,
the analogue of the
A
and
B
combinations of (
6
) is given by
(
51
), but the square root structure of the actions is completely analogous.
This fact, together with the non-linearly realized supersymmetry we investigated in section
1
,
naturally lead to the guess that the
D=4

{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric Born-Infeld (BI) theory
may also satisfy a
T^{2}
flow.
The next section is devoted to explaining how this is the case.

4
Supersymmetric Born-Infeld from Supercurrent-Squared Deformation

In Section
1
we proved, by analogy and extension of the
D=4
results of
[
Bagger:1996wp
]
, that two
D=2
supercurrent-squared flows possess additional non-linearly realized supersymmetry. In this section we reverse the logic. We will look at a well-studied model, namely the Bagger-Galperin construction
[
Bagger:1996wp
]
of
D=4
,
{\cal N}=1
Born-Infeld theory
[
Deser:1980ck
,
cecotti:1987
]
, and show that it satisfies a supercurrent-squared flow equation.

1
D=4

{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric BI and non-linear supersymmetry

Let us review some well known results about the
D=4

{\cal N}=1
Born-Infeld theory

[
cecotti:1987
]
, the
Bagger-Galperin action
[
Bagger:1996wp
]
, the non-linearly realized second supersymmetry, and its precise
{\cal N}=2\to{\cal N}=1

supersymmetry breaking pattern.
For more detail, we refer to the following references on the subject
[
Bagger:1996wp
,
Rocek:1997hi
,
Kuzenko:2015rfx
,
Antoniadis:2017jsk
,
Antoniadis:2019gbd
,
cecotti:1987
]
.

We start with the following
\mathcal{N}=2
superfield,

\mathcal{W}(y,\theta,\tilde{\theta})=X(y,\theta)+\sqrt{2}i\tilde{\theta}W(y,\theta)-\tilde{\theta}^{2}G(y,\theta)~,\qquad y^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}+i\tilde{\theta}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\tilde{\theta}}~,

(1)

which is chiral with respect to both supersymmetries:

\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{W}=\bar{\tilde{D}}_{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{W}=0~.

(2)

Since we are ultimately interested in partial
{\cal N}=2\to{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry breaking,
we will mostly use
{\cal N}=1
superfields associated to the
\theta
Grassmann variables to describe manifest supersymmetry,
while we use the
\tilde{\theta}
variable for the hidden non-linearly realized supersymmetry.
The
{\cal N}=1
superfields
X
,
W_{\alpha}
, and
G
of eq. (
1
)
are chiral under the manifest
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry. Under the additional hidden
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry, they transform as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}i\epsilon W~,

(3a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\partial_{\mu}X+\sqrt{2}i\epsilon G~,

(3b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}G

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\partial_{\mu}W\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}~.

(3c)

The superfield (
1
) has
16+16
independent off-shell components and is reducible.
It contains the degrees of freedom of an
\mathcal{N}=2
vector and tensor multiplet.
To reduce the degrees of freedom and describe an irreducible
{\cal N}=2
off-shell vector multiplet,
we impose the following conditions on the
{\cal N}=1
components of
\cal W
:

(i)

First that
W_{\alpha}
is the field-strength superfield of an
\mathcal{N}=1
vector multiplet satisfying,

D^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}-\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}=0~,

(4)

(ii)

and that

G=\frac{1}{4}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}~.

(5)

The latter condition can easily be seen to be consistent since
it is straightforward to verify
that
\frac{1}{4}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}
transforms in the same way as
G
given in (
3c
).
Therefore
we can impose (
5
)
without violating
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry.

Since
\mathcal{W}
is chiral with respect to both sets of supersymmetries, we can consider the following Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{N}=2}_{\mathcal{W}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\tilde{\theta}\,\mathcal{W}^{2}+c.c.=\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\Big(W^{2}-\frac{1}{2}X\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}\Big)+c.c.~.

(6)

On the other hand, the
\mathcal{N}=2
Maxwell theory written in terms of the
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral superfields
X
and
W_{\alpha}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{N}=2}_{\text{ Maxwell}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{X}X+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\,W^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\,\bar{W}^{2}~,

(7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\Big(W^{2}-\frac{1}{2}X\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}\Big)+c.c.+{\rm total~derivative}~.

We see that these two Lagrangians are the same, confirming that the extra constraint imposed on
\mathcal{W}
is correct.
The off-shell
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet can therefore be described in term of the following
\mathcal{N}=2
superfield

\mathcal{W}(y,\theta,\tilde{\theta})=X(y,\theta)+\sqrt{2}i\tilde{\theta}W(y,\theta)-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\theta}^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}(y,\theta)~,

(8)

where
X
and
W_{\alpha}
are
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral and vector multiplets, respectively. Their component expansion reads:

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\lambda_{\alpha}+\theta_{\alpha}{\sf D}-i(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\theta)_{\alpha}F_{\mu\nu}+\theta^{2}(\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}{\bar{\lambda}})_{\alpha}~,

(9a)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x+\sqrt{2}\theta\chi-\theta^{2}\,{\sf F}~.

(9b)

Following
[
Rocek:1997hi
]
(see also
[
Kuzenko:2015rfx
,
Antoniadis:2017jsk
,
Antoniadis:2019gbd
]
),
we break
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry by considering a Lorentz and
{\cal N}=1
invariant condensate with a non-trivial dependence on the hidden Grassmann variables
\langle\mathcal{W}\rangle=\mathcal{W}_{\text{def}}\propto\tilde{\theta}^{2}\neq 0
,
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{\text{new}}}=\langle\mathcal{W}\rangle+\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}+\mathcal{W}_{\text{def}}~,

(10a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{\text{new}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X+\sqrt{2}i\tilde{\theta}W-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\theta}^{2}\Big(\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}+\frac{2}{\kappa}\Big)~.

(10b)

The hidden supersymmetry transformations of the
{\cal N}=1
components of the deformed
{\cal N}=2
vector multiplet turn out to be

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}i\epsilon W~,

(11a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\kappa}\epsilon+\frac{i}{2\sqrt{2}}\epsilon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\bar{X}+\sqrt{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\partial_{\mu}X~.

(11b)

Assuming the model under consideration preserves the manifest
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry, which implies
\langle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}X\rangle=0
,
the explicit non-linear
\kappa
-dependent term in the transformation of the fermionic
W_{\alpha}
signals
the spontaneous partial breaking
{\cal N}=2\to{\cal N}=1
of the hidden supersymmetry.

To describe the Maxwell-Goldstone multiplet for the partial breaking
{\cal N}=2\to{\cal N}=1
, we impose the following nilpotent constraint on the deformed
{\cal N}=2
superfield
{\mathcal{W}_{\text{new}}}

[
Rocek:1997hi
]
:

({\mathcal{W}_{\text{new}}})^{2}=0~.

(12)

Once reduced to
{\cal N}=1
superfields, following the expansion (
10b
), this constraint implies
the Bagger-Galperin constraint
[
Bagger:1996wp
]

\frac{1}{\kappa}X=W^{2}-\frac{1}{2}X\bar{D}^{2}\bar{X}~,

(13)

which can be solved to eliminate
X
in terms of

W^{2}=W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}
and its complex conjugate

\bar{W}^{2}=\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}
as

X=\kappa W^{2}-\kappa^{3}\bar{D}^{2}\Big[\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}^{2}}}\Big]~.

(14)

Here we have introduced

\mathcal{A}=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}(D^{2}W^{2}+\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2})=\overline{\mathcal{A}}~,\qquad\mathcal{B}=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}(D^{2}W^{2}-\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2})=-\overline{\mathcal{B}}~.

(15)

For later use, we denote the lowest components of the composite superfields
\cal A
and
\cal B
as

A=\mathcal{A}|_{\theta=0}~,\qquad B=\mathcal{B}|_{\theta=0}~.

(16)

We will not repeat the derivation of (
14
) which can be found in the original paper

[
Bagger:1996wp
]
, and was reviewed and slightly modified in section
1
for our analysis in two dimensions.

The
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric BI action can be constructed using the following
{\cal N}=1
(anti-)chiral Lagrangian linear in
X
:

\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}=\frac{1}{4\kappa}\Big(\int d^{2}\theta X+\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\bar{X}\Big)~.

(17)

The second hidden supersymmetry eq. (
11a
) written in terms of the
unconstrained real vector multiplet
V
, where
W_{\alpha}=-1/4\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}D_{\alpha}V
, takes the form

\tilde{\delta}X=-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{2}i\epsilon^{\alpha}\bar{D}^{2}D_{\alpha}V~.

(18)

Using the fact that
D^{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}D_{\alpha}\propto\partial_{\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}D^{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\dot{\alpha}}
,
one can immediately see that the supersymmetry variation of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
in (
17
) is a total derivative.
Therefore this supersymmetric BI action is invariant under the second hidden non-linear supersymmetry.

Using the solution (
14
), the supersymmetric BI Lagrangian takes the explicit form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa}\int d^{2}\theta\Big(\kappa W^{2}-\kappa^{3}\bar{D}^{2}\Big[\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}\Big]\Big)+c.c.\,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\,W^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{W}^{2}+2\kappa^{2}\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}~,

(19)

which makes it clear that the supersymmetric BI is a non-linear deformation of the free
{\cal N}=1
Maxwell theory.
This supersymmetric extension of BI was first constructed by Bagger and Galperin in

[
Bagger:1996wp
]
. In this work when we refer to the supersymmetric BI theory, we will always
mean the Bagger-Galperin action.

We can easily calculate the flow under the
\kappa^{2}
coupling constant,

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}{\partial\kappa^{2}}=2\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}~.

(20)

Our goal is now to show that the right hand side of this flow equation on-shell
is the specific supercurrent bilinear (
14
) that we introduced earlier. This will establish a supercurrent-squared flow for the supersymmetric BI action.

Before turning to the core of this analysis
let us recall that at the leading order in
\kappa^{2}
, the fact that
D=4

{\cal N}=1
BI
satisfies a supercurrent-squared flow was already noticed in
[
cecotti:1987
]
.
This result was also highlighted recently in the introduction of
[
Chang:2018dge
]
.
In fact, note that in the free limit
\alpha=\kappa=0
, the Lagrangian (
19
) becomes
the
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric Maxwell theory.
Its supercurrent multiplet is

{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=-4W_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}~,\qquad\mathcal{X}=0~,

(21)

where
\mathcal{X}=0
because super-Maxwell theory is scale invariant.
The supersymmetric
T^{2}
deformation operator (
14
) is then simply given by

\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=\frac{1}{16}{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}{\mathcal{J}}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}-\frac{5}{8}\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}=W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}~,

(22)

and to leading order (
20
) turns into
[
cecotti:1987
]

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}{\partial\kappa^{2}}=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{2})=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{2})~.

(23)

This shows that the supercurrent-squared flow equation
is satisfied at this order.
The rest of this section is
devoted to demonstrating the full non-linear
extension of this result.
First, we are going to look at the bosonic truncation of (
19
) and (
20
).

2
Bosonic truncation

In the pure bosonic case
the gauginos are set to zero in (
9a
),
\lambda=\bar{\lambda}=0
, and
W^{2},\bar{W}^{2}
only have
\theta^{2},\bar{\theta}^{2}
components, so
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
can only contribute to the lowest components:

A=\mathcal{A}|_{\theta=0}=2\kappa^{2}\Big(F^{2}-2{\sf D}^{2}\Big)~,\qquad B=\mathcal{B}|_{\theta=0}=2\kappa^{2}iF\tilde{F}~.

(24)

Therefore the supersymmetric BI Lagrangian reduces to

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{8\kappa^{2}}\Big[1-\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}\Big(F^{2}-2{\sf D}^{2}\Big)-4\kappa^{4}\Big(F\tilde{F}\Big)^{2}}\Big]~.

(25)

The auxiliary field can be set to
{\sf D}=0
using its
{\rm EOM}
,
and the Lagrangian is then equivalent to the bosonic BI Lagrangian (
1
) with the identification
\alpha^{2}=8\kappa^{2}
.
This immediately implies that on-shell the bosonic truncation of the supersymmetric BI
satisfies a
T^{2}
flow equation driven by the
O_{T^{2}}
operator (
6
), as we discussed in (
10a
).
A similar conclusion holds for the complete supersymmetric model of (
19
) and (
20
).

3
Supersymmetric Born-Infeld as a supercurrent-squared flow

The supercurrent for the supersymmetric BI action
(
19
) was computed in
[
Kuzenko:2002vk
]
for
\kappa^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
.
To simplify notation, we will also consider the special case
\kappa^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
in our intermediate computations.
The
\kappa
-dependence can be restored easily and will appear in the final formulae.

We can straightforwardly use the results of
[
Kuzenko:2002vk
]
for our supercurrent-squared flow analysis.
The FZ multiplet was computed for a class of
models described by the following Lagrangian,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\,W^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{W}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}\Lambda(u,\bar{u})~,

(26)

where

u=\frac{1}{8}D^{2}W^{2}~,\qquad\bar{u}=\frac{1}{8}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}~.

(27)

The action (
19
) turns out to be given by the following choice of
\Lambda(u,\bar{u})

\Lambda(u,\bar{u})=\frac{4}{1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}~,

(28)

where

\mathcal{A}=2(u+\bar{u})~,\qquad\mathcal{B}=2(u-\bar{u})~.

(29)

Following
[
Kuzenko:2002vk
]
, we also introduce the composite superfields

\Gamma(u,\bar{u})=\frac{\partial(u\Lambda)}{\partial u}~,\qquad\bar{\Gamma}(u,\bar{u})=\frac{\partial(\bar{u}\Lambda)}{\partial\bar{u}}~,

(30)

which, in the case of interest to us where (
28
) holds, satisfy

\displaystyle\Gamma+\bar{\Gamma}-\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}~,

(31a)

\displaystyle\bar{u}\Gamma+u\bar{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}~.

(31b)

The supercurrents will also be functionals of the following composite

\displaystyle iM_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}\Bigg[1-\frac{1}{4}\bar{D}^{2}\Bigg(\bar{W}^{2}\Big(\Lambda+\frac{1}{8}D^{2}(W^{2}\frac{\partial\Lambda}{\partial u})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg]~,

(32a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}\Big(1-2\bar{u}\Gamma\Big)+W\bar{W}(\cdots)+W^{2}(\cdots)~,

(32b)

where
W\bar{W}(\cdots)
denotes terms which are proportional to
W_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}
,
while
W^{2}(\cdots)
denotes terms proportional to
W^{2}
.
We will use similar notation with ellipses denoting quantities with bare fermionic terms that will not contribute to the calculation because of nilpotency conditions.

With the ingredients introduced above, the FZ multiplet for
the supersymmetric BI action is given by

[
Kuzenko:2002vk
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\Big(\bar{W}^{2}(\Gamma+\bar{\Gamma}-\Lambda)\Big)~,

(33a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2iM_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}+2iW_{\alpha}\bar{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{12}[D_{\alpha},\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}]\Big(W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}\Big)\cdot\Big(\Gamma+\bar{\Gamma}-\Lambda\Big)

(33b)

\displaystyle+W^{2}\bar{W}(\cdots)+\bar{W}^{2}W(\cdots)~.

For our purposes, the superfields
X
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}
can be further simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}\cdot\Big(\Gamma+\bar{\Gamma}-\Lambda\Big)+W^{2}\bar{W}(\cdots)~,

(34a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{3\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)}+W^{2}\bar{W}(\cdots)~,

(34b)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4W_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}(1-\bar{u}\Gamma-u\bar{\Gamma})+\frac{1}{12}[D_{\alpha},\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}]\Big(W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}\Big)\cdot\Big(\Gamma+\bar{\Gamma}-\Lambda\Big)

(35b)

\displaystyle+W^{2}\bar{W}(\cdots)+\bar{W}^{2}W(\cdots)~,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4W_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}+\frac{2D_{\alpha}W^{2}\cdot\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{2}}{3\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}}

\displaystyle+W^{2}\bar{W}(\cdots)+\bar{W}^{2}W(\cdots)~,

where we used (
31a
).

The computation of
\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}
is trivial and receives contributions only from the square of the first term in (
34b
).
The computation of
\mathcal{J}^{2}
is less trivial.
It is obvious that the last two complicated terms in the second line of (
35b
) make no contribution since all the terms are
proportional to
W\bar{W}
, and we have the nilpotency property
W_{\alpha}W_{\beta}W_{\gamma}=0
.
The square of the first term is easy to compute, and it is proportional to
W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}
.
Next we consider the product between the first and second term in (
35b
) which leads to the relation:

W_{\alpha}\bar{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}\cdot D^{\alpha}W^{2}\cdot\bar{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{2}=W^{2}(DW)\cdot\bar{W}^{2}(\bar{D}\bar{W})=0~.

(36)

Remarkably, this cross term vanishes, as shown in Appendix
7.F
. Therefore, we have the on-shell relation that

W^{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muW\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}DW=0~.

(37)

A simple physical interpretation of this condition is that the manifest
supersymmetry is preserved on-shell, implying that the auxiliary field
{\sf D}\propto D^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}|_{\theta=0}

has no vev, and is at least linear in gaugino fields
\lambda_{\alpha}\propto W_{\alpha}|_{\theta=0}
.
The vanishing of this cross term can be compared with the pure bosonic case where the cross terms in
T^{2}

vanish because of the tracelessness property of the free Maxwell stress tensor; see section
3
.
Finally, we compute the square of the second term in (
35b
) which includes the following structure:

D^{\alpha}W^{2}\cdot\bar{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{2}\cdot D_{\alpha}W^{2}\cdot\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{W}^{2}=W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}D^{2}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}~.

(38)

Here we have used
(D_{\alpha}W_{\beta})(D^{\alpha}W^{\beta})=-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}W^{2}+W^{\beta}D^{2}W_{\beta}

to simplify the result.

In summary, on-shell the contributions to the supercurrent-squared operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{2}}

defined in eq. (
14
)
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\Bigg\{\frac{16W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}^{2}}+\frac{4W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}D^{2}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{9\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}^{2}\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{9}\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}D^{2}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}^{2}\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)^{2}}\,.

(40)

Adding these results gives the supersymmetric
T^{2}
primary operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{2}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathcal{J}^{2}+\frac{5}{4}\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}^{2}}\Bigg(1-\frac{D^{2}W^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{4\Big(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)^{2}}\Bigg)\,,

\displaystyle=\frac{W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{\mathcal{A}^{2}-\mathcal{B}^{2}}{(1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}})^{2}}\Big)\,

\displaystyle=\frac{2W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big({1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)}}\,.

(41)

It is worth noting that the simplifications occurring in constructing
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{2}}
from the supercurrents
are very similar to the bosonic case of (
10a
).

Comparing with (
20
), we see that
eq. (
41
) proves that the supersymmetric BI action (
19
)
is an on-shell solution of the flow equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}}{\partial\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\,\frac{2W^{2}\bar{W}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big({1+\mathcal{A}+\sqrt{1+2\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}^{2}}\Big)}}~,

(42a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\,\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}+\text{total derivatives}+{\rm EOM}~.

It therefore describes a supercurrent-squared deformation
of the
{\cal N}=1
free Maxwell Lagrangian.
This result establishes a relationship between non-linearly realized supersymmetry and
supercurrent-squared flow equations in
D=4
. We note that this analysis can be extended to the supersymmetric ModMax theory, whose supercurrent-squared deformation is the corresponding supersymmetric ModMax-BI theory
[
Ferko:2022iru
]
.

Before closing
this section, we should make a few comments regarding the on-shell condition (
37
)
used in establishing the
supercurrent-squared flow equation for the
D=4

{\cal N}=1
BI action.
First it is important to stress that the flow equation is not satisfied by the supersymmetric BI action off-shell.
Second, we note that the specific combination of
\mathcal{J}^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}
studied
is the unique choice for which (
19
) satisfies a supercurrent-squared flow equation, even if only on-shell.

Such a non-trivial condition satisfied by the on-shell supersymmetric BI action is intriguing and hints at the existence
of appropriate (super)field redefinitions which might
lead to a different supersymmetric extension of BI that satisfies the flow equation
off-shell. For example, it is know that the dependence of the off-shell extension on the auxiliary field
{\sf D}

can be modified by appropriate (super)field redefinitions, as well as redefinitions of the full superspace Lagrangian.
We refer to
[
Cecotti:1986gb
,
GonzalezRey:1998kh
,
Kuzenko:2011tj
,
Bagger:1997pi
]
for a list of relevant papers on this subject.
Under field redefinitions, the hidden supersymmetry will be modified but will remain a non-linearly realized symmetry of the
theory. The existence of an off-shell solution of the supercurrent-squared flow is an interesting question for future research.

5
D=4
Goldstino Action From Supercurrent-Squared Deformation

In section
4
we showed that the Bagger-Galperin action for the
D=4
,
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetric BI
theory satisfies a supercurrent-squared flow. It is known that the truncation of this model to fermions describes
a Goldstino action for
D=4
,
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry breaking; see, for example,
[
Hatanaka:2003cr
,
Kuzenko:2005wh
,
Kuzenko:2011tj
]
.
The
{\cal N}=1
non-linearly
realized supersymmetry arises as the non-linearly realized part of the
{\cal N}=2\to{\cal N}=1

breaking of the supersymmetric BI.
We have shown in sections
3
and
2

that the bosonic truncation of the supersymmetric BI satisfies a
T^{2}
flow equation.
The same should be true for the fermionic truncation.
More generally, one might argue that
D=4

{\cal N}=1
Goldstino models could satisfy a sort of flow equation that organizes their
expansion in the supersymmetry breaking scale parameter.

Note that in the
D=2
case, the intuition is similar. If we consider the actions analyzed in section
1

that describe Goldstone models for partial
D=2

{\cal N}=(4,4)\to{\cal N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry breaking,
one can immediately argue that their fermionic truncation describes Goldstino actions possessing non-linearly realized
D=2

{\cal N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry. These, by construction, are expected to satisfy a
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-flow equation.
In fact,
such an argument is in agreement
with the very nice recent analysis of
[
Cribiori:2019xzp
]

where a
D=2
Goldstino model possessing
{\cal N}=(2,2)
non-linearly realized supersymmetry
was shown to satisfy the supercurrent-squared flow equation for the FZ multiplet, which was given in (
10
) of Chapter
3
.
6
6

6

We refer to

[
Farakos:2016zam
]
for a discussion of various models possessing non-linearly realized
(2,2)
supersymmetry.

The model analyzed in
[
Cribiori:2019xzp
]

is the analogue of the
D=4
model of
[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
,
Komargodski:2009rz
]
and related on-shell to
the Goldstino model of
[
Rocek:1978nb
]
.
7
7

7

Note that the Goldstino models of

[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
,
Komargodski:2009rz
,
Rocek:1978nb
]

were shown in
[
Kuzenko:2005wh
,
Kuzenko:2011tj
]
to be identical to the
fermionic truncation of the supersymmetric BI action up to a field redefinition of the Goldstino.

This section is devoted to showing that these
D=4

{\cal N}=1
Goldstino models
satisfy a supercurrent-squared flow driven by the operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{2}}
of the supersymmetric BI, in agreement with
the arguments given above.

1
D=4
Goldstino actions

The Volkov-Akulov (VA) action is the low energy description of supersymmetry breaking. There are several representations of the
Goldstino action that are equivalent to the Volkov-Akulov form; see
[
Kuzenko:2011tj
,
Cribiori:2016hdz
]
for comprehensive discussions.
Here we will focus on two models, but we start by reviewing a few general features of Goldstino actions.

The original VA action was obtained by requiring its invariance under the the non-linear supersymmetry
transformation
[
Volkov:1973ix
]

\delta_{\xi}\lambda^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\kappa}\xi^{\alpha}-i\kappa(\lambda\sigma^{m}\bar{\xi}-\xi\sigma^{m}\bar{\lambda})\partial_{m}\lambda^{\alpha}~.

(1)

Explicitly, the original Lagrangian was proven to be

\mathcal{L}_{\rm VA}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\det A=-\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}-\frac{i}{2}(\lambda\sigma^{m}\partial_{m}\bar{\lambda}-\partial_{m}\lambda\sigma^{m}\bar{\lambda})+\text{interactions}~,

(2)

where

A_{m}{}^{a}=\delta_{m}{}^{a}-i\kappa^{2}\partial_{m}\lambda\sigma^{a}\bar{\lambda}+i\kappa^{2}\lambda\sigma^{a}\partial_{m}\bar{\lambda}~.

(3)

The alternative representation of the Goldstino action
that interests us
was originally introduced by Casalbuoni
et al.
in
[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
]
,
and later rediscovered and made fashionable
by Komargodski and Seiberg
[
Komargodski:2009rz
]
.
This model, which following recent literature we will call the KS model,
was constructed by imposing nilpotent superfield constraints as a generalization of Roček’s
seminal ideas for the Goldstino model described in
[
Rocek:1978nb
]
.
After integrating out an auxiliary field in the KS model, described in more detail in the next section,
the explicit form of the Lagrangian is given by
the following very simple combination of terms:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{KS}}=-f^{2}-\frac{i}{2}(\psi\sigma^{m}\partial_{m}\bar{\psi}-\partial_{m}\psi\sigma^{m}\bar{\psi})-\frac{1}{4f^{2}}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\psi}^{2}\partial_{\mu}\psi^{2}-\frac{1}{16f^{6}}\psi^{2}\bar{\psi}^{2}\partial^{2}\psi^{2}\partial^{2}\bar{\psi}^{2}~.

(4)

The action is invariant under a quite involved non-linearly realized supersymmetry transformation whose
explicit form can be found in
[
Kuzenko:2010ef
,
Kuzenko:2005wh
]
.
The Goldstino actions described by (
2
) and (
4
)
prove to be equivalent off-shell up to a field redefinition
[
Kuzenko:2010ef
,
Kuzenko:2005wh
]
.

2
D=4
KS Goldstino model as a supercurrent-squared flow

The goal in the rest of this section is to straightforwardly generalize the analysis of
[
Cribiori:2019xzp
]
to
D=4
and to show how the KS action satisfies a flow equation
arising from a
T^{2}
deformation of the free fermion action.

KS model

Let us start by reviewing the Goldstino model of
[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
,
Komargodski:2009rz
]
.
Consider the following Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\rm KS}=\int d^{4}\theta\,\bar{\Phi}\Phi+\int d^{2}\theta\Big(f\Phi+\frac{1}{2}\Lambda\Phi^{2}\Big)+\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\Big(f\bar{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\Lambda}\bar{\Phi}^{2}\Big)~,

(5)

where
\Phi,\,\bar{\Phi}
are
D=4

{\cal N}=1
chiral and anti-chiral superfields, satisfying the constraints
\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\Phi=D_{\alpha}\bar{\Phi}=0
. The constant parameter
f
, which describes the supersymmetry breaking scale,
is real. The superfields
\Lambda,\,\bar{\Lambda}
are chiral and anti-chiral
Lagrange multipliers whose
{\rm EOM}
yield the nilpotent constraints

\Phi^{2}=\bar{\Phi}^{2}=0~.

(6)

The equation of motion for
\Phi
is

\frac{1}{4}\bar{D}^{2}\bar{\Phi}=\Lambda\Phi+f~,\qquad\frac{1}{4}D^{2}\Phi=\bar{\Lambda}\bar{\Phi}+f~.

(7)

As a consequence, we also have

\Phi\bar{D}^{2}\bar{\Phi}=4f\Phi~,\qquad\bar{\Phi}D^{2}\Phi=4f\bar{\Phi}~,

(8)

where the nilpotent properties of (
6
) are used.
Note that the constraints (
6
) and (
8
) are the ones originally used by Roček to define
his Goldstino model
[
Rocek:1978nb
]
. These observations make manifest the on-shell equivalence of the KS model with Roček’s Goldstino model in a simple superspace setting.
The off-shell equivalence of all these Golstino models up to field redefinitions, including the VA action, was proven in

[
Kuzenko:2011tj
]
.

The Lagrange multiplier in (
5
) imposes the nilpotent constraint
\Phi^{2}=0
on the chiral superfield
\Phi
.
This condition can be solved in terms of the spinor field
\psi
and the auxiliary field
F
of the chiral multiplet,

[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
,
Komargodski:2009rz
]
:

\Phi=\frac{\psi^{2}}{2F}+\sqrt{2}\theta\psi+\theta^{2}F~,

(9)

which is sensible assuming that
F\neq 0
.
Substituting back into (
5
) gives a Lagrangian expressed in terms of
\psi
and the auxiliary field
F
,

{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm KS}=-\frac{i}{2}\psi\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}F+\frac{1}{8}\frac{\bar{\psi}^{2}}{\bar{F}}\partial^{2}\Big(\frac{\psi^{2}}{F}\Big)+fF+c.c.~.

(10)

The auxiliary field can then be eliminated using its equation of motion, which can be solved in closed form

F=-f\Bigg(1+\frac{\bar{\psi}^{2}}{4f^{4}}\partial^{2}\psi^{2}-\frac{3}{16f^{8}}\psi^{2}\bar{\psi}^{2}\partial^{2}\psi^{2}\partial^{2}\bar{\psi}^{2}\Bigg)~,

(11)

together with the complex conjugate expression for
\bar{F}
.
Plugging (
11
) into (
10
)
gives the Goldstino action (
4
)

[
Casalbuoni:1988xh
,
Komargodski:2009rz
]
.

D=4
Goldstino action as a supercurrent-squared flow

One advantage of using the KS model compared to other Goldstino actions is the relatively simple form of the action, thanks to the Lagrange multiplier, which makes the computation of its supercurrent easier.
The FZ multiplet resulting from the action (
5
) is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=2D_{\alpha}\Phi\cdot\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\Phi}-\frac{2}{3}[D_{\alpha},\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}](\Phi\bar{\Phi})=\frac{2}{3}D_{\alpha}\Phi\cdot\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\Phi}-\frac{2i}{3}\Big(\Phi\partial_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\Phi}-\bar{\Phi}\partial_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\Phi\Big)\,,

(12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=4\Big(f\Phi+\frac{1}{2}\Lambda\Phi^{2}\Big)-\frac{1}{3}\bar{D}^{2}(\Phi\bar{\Phi})=\frac{8}{3}f\Phi+2\Lambda\Phi^{2}\,.

(13)

The composite operators
{\mathcal{J}}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

and

\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}

are then

{\mathcal{J}}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{64}{9}f^{2}\Phi\bar{\Phi}+\text{total derivatives}+{\rm EOM}~,

(14)

and

\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}=\frac{64}{9}f^{2}\Phi\bar{\Phi}+{\rm EOM}~,

(15)

where we used (
6
) and (
7
).
The supercurrent-squared operator (
14
) then takes the form

\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=\frac{1}{16}{\mathcal{J}}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}-\frac{5}{8}\mathcal{X}\bar{\mathcal{X}}=-4f^{2}\Phi\bar{\Phi}+{\rm EOM}+\text{total derivatives}~.

(16)

The descendant operator
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}
of eq. (
15
) becomes

{\sf O}_{T^{2}}=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=-4f^{2}\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\Phi\bar{\Phi}=2f^{3}\int d^{2}\theta\,\Phi+2f^{3}\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{\Phi}~,

(17)

where we used (
8
) in the last equality.

From (
5
), it is easy to see that the following relation holds:

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\rm KS}}{\partial f}=\int d^{2}\theta\,\Phi+\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{\Phi}~.

(18)

By identifying the coupling constants,

\gamma=-\frac{1}{4f^{2}}~,

(19)

it follows immediately that the KS action,

S_{\gamma}=\int d^{4}x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm KS}~,

(20)

satisfies the flow equation

\frac{\partial S_{\gamma}}{\partial\gamma}=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta\,\Phi+\int d^{4}xd^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\bar{\Phi}=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}=\int d^{4}x\,{\sf O}_{T^{2}}~.

(21)

This proves that
(
5
) satisfies a supercurrent-squared flow
(or
T^{2}
flow) equation.
Because on-shell the actions (
4
) and (
5
) are equivalent,
and the equation

\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\;\mathcal{O}_{T^{2}}={\sf O}_{T^{2}}

holds,
eq. (
21
) proves that the
D=4

{\cal N}=1
Goldstino action arises from a supercurrent-squared deformation.
8
8

8

The careful reader may find that the flow can also be satisfied by other supercurrent-squared operators,
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}-r\bar{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{X}
, with arbitrary
r
because of the linearity between
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
and
\bar{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{X}
. It is worth pointing out the same thing happens in
D=2

[
Cribiori:2019xzp
]
. We stress that this is not the case for the supercurrent-squared flow satisfied by the
D=4
supersymmetric Born-Infeld action.

6
Higher Dimensions and Connections to Amplitudes

In this thesis, we have primarily focused on two-dimensional quantum field theories, where
{T\overline{T}}
and related supercurrent-squared operators can be unambiguously defined at the quantum level. For
D>2
, there appears to be no complete argument showing that any of the proposed operators

O_{T^{2}}^{[r]}
of eq. (
1
), including the holographic operator of
[
Taylor:2018xcy
,
Hartman:2018tkw
]
, possess any particularly nice quantum properties.

However, despite the absence of a well-defined quantum operator in higher dimensions, the connection between non-linearly realized symmetries and our
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
example suggests that operators of this form still have some special properties (at least at the classical level). It would be very interesting to understand whether any supersymmetric completion of our descendant operator
{\sf O}_{T^{2}}
of (
15
) could provide a well-defined deformation at the quantum level. Such an extension would necessarily involve contributions from other current-squared type operators which do not vanish on-shell. This possibility seems most promising in theories with extended, and perhaps even maximal, supersymmetry.

One interesting direction for future investigation concerns the relationship between
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
deformations and amplitudes. In two dimensions,
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
simply modifies the
S
-matrix of the undeformed theory by a CDD factor
[
Dubovsky:2013ira
]
, but one might wonder about the
S
-matrices of higher-dimensional theories deformed by generalizations of
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. One hint is that theories with non-linearly realized symmetries exhibit enhanced soft behavior – indeed, in the case of non-linearly realized
super
symmetry, there is a proof that such symmetries generically lead to constraints on the soft behavior of the
S
-matrix
[
Kallosh:2016qvo
]
. This fact which has been applied to the Volkov-Akulov action
[
Kallosh:2016lwj
]
, which also satisfies a
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-like flow.

There are also examples involving purely bosonic theories. For instance, in four dimensions, the Dirac action is the unique Lorentz-invariant Lagrangian for a single scalar which is consistent with factorization, has one derivative per field, and exhibits soft degree
\sigma=2
for its scattering amplitudes
[
Cheung:2014dqa
]
. Similarly, it has been shown that the Born-Infeld action for a vector can be fixed by demanding enhanced soft behavior in a particular multi-soft limit
[
Cheung:2018oki
]
, which can be understood in the context of T-duality and dimensional reduction
[
Elvang:2019twd
]
. Given the hints of a deeper relationship between supercurrent-squared deformations, non-linearly realized symmetries, and actions of Dirac or Born-Infeld type, it is natural to ask whether such deformations enhance the soft behavior of scattering amplitudes in a more general context.

Chapter 5
{T\overline{T}}
and Non-Abelian Gauge Theory

In this chapter, we will define a theory for a non-abelian gauge field in two dimensions using the
{T\overline{T}}
operator, following the discussion in “A Non-Abelian Analogue of DBI from
{T\overline{T}}
”
[
Brennan:2019azg
]
.

Although we focus on the bosonic formulation of
{T\overline{T}}
rather than the supercurrent-squared deformations developed in the preceding chapters, the gauge theory which we define here is compatible with maximal supersymmetry. This deformed theory shares some properties with the Dirac-Born-Infeld theory which describes gauge fields living on a brane, although it does not reduce to DBI even in the abelian case. We begin by reviewing some facts and motivation about brane physics.

1
Background on Brane Physics

Imagine a
p
-brane embedded in an ambient
(D+1)
-dimensional Minkowski space-time. Any such brane spontaneously breaks the Poincaré symmetry of the ambient space-time:

ISO(D,1)\,\rightarrow\,ISO(p,1).

(1)

In particular, the breaking of translational symmetry guarantees the existence of
D-p
universal scalar fields on the brane world-volume, collectively denoted
\phi
, which are Nambu-Goldstone (NG) bosons for the broken translations. The physics of these modes is governed by the Dirac action,

S_{\rm Dirac}=-T_{p}\int d^{p+1}\sigma\,\sqrt{-\det\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\right)},

(2)

with brane world-volume coordinates
\sigma
and a single dimensionful parameter
T_{p}
. The form of this action is fixed by the broken Lorentz symmetries, which are non-linearly realized. There might also be a function of any additional non-universal scalar fields multiplying this form, which we will not consider here. This action can also equivalently be viewed as the Nambu-Goto action for the brane in static gauge.

The Born-Infeld action, on the other hand, defines a non-linear interacting extension of Maxwell theory with action:

S_{\rm BI}=-T_{p}\int d^{p+1}\sigma\,\sqrt{-\det\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}+\alpha F_{\mu\nu}\right)}.

(3)

One of the striking physical differences between Born-Infeld theory and Maxwell theory is the existence of a critical electric field determined by the dimensionful parameter
\alpha
. In string theory, Born-Infeld theory describes the leading interactions for the gauge-field supported on a D-brane
[
Abouelsaood:1986gd
]
. In that context both
T_{p}
and
\alpha
are fixed in terms of the fundamental string tension
\alpha^{\prime}
with
\alpha=2\pi\alpha^{\prime}.

The combined Dirac-Born-Infeld (DBI) action is a complete description of the physics of a single D-brane at leading order in string perturbation theory, and under the assumption that acceleration terms like
\partial F
or
\partial^{2}\phi
are negligible:

S_{\rm DBI}=-T_{p}\int d^{p+1}\sigma\,\sqrt{-\det\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+\alpha F_{\mu\nu}\right)}.

(4)

For multiple coincident branes, the abelian gauge symmetry is replaced by a non-abelian symmetry, and the fields
(\phi,F)
typically take values in the adjoint representation of the gauge group. We will not assume any particular representation for the scalar fields in this discussion. It is natural to pose the following long considered question: what might replace
(\ref {DBI_nonabelian})
in the non-abelian theory? For the scalar fields appearing in the induced metric of the Dirac action
(\ref {Dirac})
, one could easily imagine making the replacement

\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\,\rightarrow\,\Tr\left(D_{\mu}\phi D_{\nu}\phi\right),

(5)

where
\phi
is now matrix-valued and
D
is an appropriate covariant derivative. For the Born-Infeld action of
(\ref {BI_nonabelian})
, however, an interesting gauge-invariant replacement of this sort is not possible. In
(\ref {replace})

\Tr
denotes the trace over gauge indices. When needed, we will use
\tr
to denote the trace over Lorentz indices so that,

\tr(F^2)=F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\mu}.

(6)

Indeed what one means by the Born-Infeld approximation, namely neglecting acceleration terms like
DF
, is ambiguous. Unlike the abelian case,

\commutator{D_\m}{D_\n}=-iF_{\mu\nu},

(7)

so there is no clear cut way of truncating the full brane effective action by throwing out acceleration terms.

With considerable hard work there is, however, some data known about brane couplings beyond the two derivative non-abelian kinetic terms
\Tr(F_{\m\n}F^{\m\n})
at leading order in string perturbation theory. This information is very nicely summarized in the thesis
[
Koerber:2004ze
]
to which we refer for a more complete discussion. For comparative purposes with our analysis, we note that the known
F^{4}
terms are correctly captured by a symmetrized trace prescription
[
Tseytlin:1997csa
,
Bergshoeff:2001dc
]
. Up to overall scaling, they are given by

{\rm STr}\left(\tr F^{4}-\frac{1}{4}(\tr F^{2})^{2}\right),

(8)

with

{\rm STr}\left(T_{1}T_{2}\ldots T_{n}\right)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\rm\sigma\in S_{n}}\Tr(T_{\s(1)} T_{\s(2)} \ldots T_{\s(n)}).

(9)

This prescription is known to fail for higher derivative terms. What is important for us is that
(\ref {f4terms})
defines a single trace operator.

To define a non-abelian analogue of the abelian DBI theory
(\ref {DBI_nonabelian})
, our approach will be to
{T\overline{T}}
deform a non-abelian gauge theory with scalar matter in two dimensions. In contrast to the preceding parts of this thesis, in this chapter we will restrict to bosonic theories for simplicity. A priori this approach has no connection to either brane physics or string theory. We will do this in steps by first recalling known results about deforming free scalars and Maxwell fields
[
Cavaglia:2016oda
,
Bonelli:2018kik
,
Conti:2018jho
]
, and then extending to charged matter and non-abelian gauge theories. Other than also involving an infinite collection of irrelevant operators, the reason this approach should be viewed as giving a non-abelian analogue of DBI is that the
{T\overline{T}}
deformation of a free scalar with parameter
\lambda
already gives the Dirac action, as we reviewed in Chapter
1
:

S_{\lambda}=\int d^{2}\sigma\frac{1}{2\lambda}\left(\sqrt{1+4\lambda|\partial\phi|^{2}}-1\right)~.

(10)

This direct connection with brane physics is reason enough to suspect that
{T\overline{T}}
applied to gauge-theory will give further insight into brane physics.

The
{T\overline{T}}
deformation of Maxwell theory, however, is already different from the Born-Infeld theory of
(\ref {BI_nonabelian})
. This is the reason we call the
T\bar{T}
-deformed theory an analogue rather than a generalization of DBI; it does not reduce to DBI even in the case of abelian gauge theory. The couplings are not given by the square-root structure of a relativistic particle but rather by a hypergeometric function
[
Conti:2018jho
]
. This might not seem very exciting in two dimensions where pure gauge fields have no propagating degrees of freedom, but that is no longer the case when we add scalar fields, even in the abelian setting. For interesting recent discussions of
{T\overline{T}}
-deformed gauge theories, see
[
Santilli:2018xux
,
Ireland:2019vvj
]
.

For the non-abelian theory defined using
{T\overline{T}}
, the
O(F^{4})
terms are already very different from what is known about non-abelian BI theory. Rather than involving a single trace operator like
(\ref {f4terms})
, they involve double trace operators. The
{T\overline{T}}
-deformed theory is quite remarkable because it has the following properties:

•

The theory is compatible with supersymmetry
[
Baggio:2018rpv
,
Chang:2018dge
,
Jiang:2019hux
,
Chang:2019kiu
,
Coleman:2019dvf
,
Ferko:2019oyv
,
Jiang:2019trm
]
. Indeed, we have discussed at length in Chapters
2
and
3
that
{T\overline{T}}

always
preserves degeneracies in energy levels like those implied by supersymmetry, and that one can often make the supersymmetry manifest via a superspace flow construction. In particular, if one
{T\overline{T}}
deforms a maximally supersymmetric starting theory then this supersymmetry is preserved!

•

The theory is believed to exist at the quantum level, unlike DBI which is an effective theory with our present level of understanding.

•

The theory has a critical electric field like BI.

This is already quite surprising in the abelian case with uncharged matter. Folklore suggests that some of these properties, like compatibility with maximal supersymmetry, should only have been true for DBI.
Indeed there are no obvious reasons that the structures seen here should not emerge from string theory, either in a closed or open string setting. In fact, the
D=10
space-time effective action for the type I/heterotic strings does contain a double trace
F^{4}
term, which is required for anomaly cancelation in
D=10
, or more generally required by supersymmetry
[
Bergshoeff:1988nn
]
. In the heterotic string, the term arises at tree-level and takes the schematic form:

S_{\rm het}\sim\int d^{10}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}\left(\Tr F^{2}\right)^{2}+\ldots.

(11)

In the dual type I frame, relevant for a brane picture, the same coupling arises from diagrams with Euler characteristic
-1

[
Tseytlin:1995fy
,
Tseytlin:1995bi
]
. This leads us to suspect that the
{T\overline{T}}
flow equation is connected with corrections to two-dimensional beta functions from higher orders in string perturbation theory.

As a final comment, we note in passing that a different way of deforming a
2d
gauge theory is via holography. For instance,
2d
JT gravity can be written in gauge theory variables as a BF theory, and one can then
{T\overline{T}}
-deform the
(0+1)
-dimensional dual to this BF theory. The bulk interpretation of such a deformation is explored in
[
us:gravity
]
.

2
Deforming Pure Gauge Theory

Before we go on to solve the
{T\overline{T}}
equation to find a non-abelian analogue of the DBI action, we will first illustrate the above techniques by solving for the
{T\overline{T}}
-deformed Yang-Mills theory. That is, we begin with an undeformed Lagrangian of the form

\displaystyle k\mathcal{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=\,F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{\mu\nu}_{a}=\,\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right)

(1)

where we will often suppress the trace for convenience and simply write
F^{2}
for
\Tr( F^2 )
, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=\frac{1}{k}F^{2}.

(2)

We retain an overall dimensionless constant
k
in the Lagrangian; in most of the calculations that follow, which we will suppress factors of
k
by setting
k=1
. The value of
k
does not affect the equations of motion associated with the action (
2
), but the sign of
k
will be important for determining critical value of the field strength
F^{2}
. To see the maximum allowed electric field for the deformed theory in Minkowski signature, we will find that we must take
k<0
so that the undeformed action is positive (however, all of our other results are valid in either Minkowski or Euclidean signature). We will restore factors of
k
, replacing
F^{2}\to\frac{1}{k}F^{2}
, when the sign is relevant.

Our goal is to find the deformed Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=f(\lambda,F^{2})
which solves the flow equation

\partial_{\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}={\rm det}\,T_{\mu\nu}~,

(3)

with initial condition
\mathcal{L}(0)=\mathcal{L}_{0}
.

It is first convenient to rewrite this flow equation using the fact that
2\times 2
matrices
M
satisfy

\displaystyle\det( M )=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\tr M\right)^{2}-\tr\left(M^{2}\right)\right)\,.

(4)

Thus we can write the
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
flow equation in a manifestly Lorentz-invariant way as

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\right)^{2}-T^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}\right).

(5)

Notice that the stress-energy tensor is a single-trace operator in the undeformed theory. In the
\lambda
expansion, the leading order deformation of the Lagrangian is therefore automatically a double trace operator. Including further corrections in
\lambda
will only generate higher order multi-trace operators. In particular – as we show in Appendix
7.G
and discuss in more detail in Chapter
6
– a single-trace deformation like the leading
F^{4}
terms of non-abelian DBI in
(\ref {f4terms})
will never be generated from a
{T\overline{T}}
flow beginning from an undeformed Lagrangian which is only a function of
F^{2}
.

The details of the calculation of the stress tensor components
T_{\mu\nu}^{(\lambda)}
for the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}(\lambda,F^{2})
are presented in Appendix
7.G
, where we find the
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
operator for an arbitrary Lagrangian depending on a field strength
F_{\mu\nu}
and a complex scalar
\phi
. We can set the scalar
\phi
to zero in the result of that Appendix to find the flow equation for a pure gauge field Lagrangian. The result, using the shorthand notation
x=F^{2}
, is

\displaystyle\frac{df}{d\lambda}=f(x)^{2}-4f(x)x\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}+4x^{2}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right)^{2}.

(6)

Next we will present several methods for solving (
6
):

1.

by directly solving the flow equation (
5
) in a series expansion;

2.

by writing the solution implicitly in terms of a complete integral;

3.

and by dualizing the field strength
F^{2}
to a scalar, deforming, and dualizing back.

The same three methods will also be applied to the case of a gauge field coupled to scalars in Section
3
.

1
Series solution of flow equation

The differential equation (
6
) derived in the preceding subsection can be brought into a simpler form by refining our ansatz to

\displaystyle f(\lambda,F^{2})=F^{2}g(\lambda F^{2})

(7)

for some new function
g
. For convenience, we define the dimensionless variable
\chi=\lambda F^{2}=\lambda x
. Then the function
g
satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial\chi}=\left(g(\chi)+2\chi g^{\prime}(\chi)\right)^{2}~.

(8)

One can solve this differential equation by making a series ansatz of the form
g(\chi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\chi^{n}
, determining the first several coefficients
c_{n}
. To order
\chi^{6}
, the function
g(\chi)
is given by

\displaystyle g(\chi)=1+\chi+3\chi^{2}+13\chi^{3}+68\chi^{4}+399\chi^{5}+2530\chi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{7}).

(9)

To determine the generating function, one can refer to an encyclopedia of integer sequences
[
hypergeometricSequence
]
to find that
g
can be written as a generalized hypergeometric function,

\displaystyle g(\chi)={}_{4}F_{3}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},1,\frac{5}{4};\frac{4}{3},\frac{5}{3},2;\frac{256}{27}\cdot\chi\right)~.

(10)

Thus the full solution for the deformed Lagrangian can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=F^{2}\cdot{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},1,\frac{5}{4};\frac{4}{3},\frac{5}{3},2;\frac{256}{27}\cdot\lambda F^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4\lambda}\left({}_{3}F_{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4};\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3};\frac{256}{27}\cdot\lambda F^{2}\right)-1\right).

(11)

The functions on the first and second lines of (
11
) are equivalent because of a hypergeometric functional identity. We will use the expression in the first line, written in terms of
{}_{4}F_{3}
rather than
{}_{3}F_{2}
, but we include the second expression to make contact with the work of
[
Conti:2018jho
]
, where this expression was first derived. We also note that the function (
11
) has appeared in an analogue of
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
defined for
(0+1)
dimensional theories
[
Gross:2019ach
]
.

2
Implicit solution

Later it will be useful to solve the
{T\overline{T}}
flow equation using a different method. We begin with the differential equation (
6
), but this time we make the ansatz

\displaystyle f(\lambda,F^{2})=\frac{1}{\lambda}h(\lambda F^{2}).

(12)

As before, we define the dimensionless variable
\chi=\lambda F^{2}
. In terms of
h
, the differential equation becomes

\displaystyle 4\chi^{2}\left(h^{\prime}(\chi)\right)^{2}-4\chi h(\chi)h^{\prime}(\chi)-\chi h^{\prime}(\chi)+h(\chi)^{2}+h(\chi)=0.

(13)

Equation (
13
) is quadratic in
h^{\prime}(\chi)
, so we can solve to find

\displaystyle\frac{dh}{d\chi}=\frac{1+4h(\chi)-\sqrt{1-8h(\chi)}}{8\chi},

(14)

where we have chosen the root which makes
h^{\prime}(\chi)
finite as
\chi\to 0
, assuming
\lim_{\chi\to 0}h(\chi)=0
.

We may separate variables in (
14
) to write

\displaystyle\int\frac{8\,dh}{1+4h(\chi)-\sqrt{1-8h(\chi)}}=\int\frac{d\chi}{\chi}.

(15)

The integrals can be evaluated in terms of logarithms; exponentiating both sides then yields

\displaystyle\chi=C\left(1-\sqrt{1-8h}\right)\left(3+\sqrt{1-8h}\right)^{3}.

(16)

Equation (
16
) implicitly defines the solution
h(\chi)
to the
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
flow equation via the roots of an algebraic equation.

We note that, choosing
C=\frac{1}{256}
, equation (
16
) is consistent with the solution derived in the previous section. Recall that the two ansatzes we made here and in subsection (
1
) are related by

\displaystyle f(\lambda,F^{2})=F^{2}f(\chi)=\frac{1}{\lambda}h(\chi),

(17)

so
h(\chi)=\chi f(\chi)
. Indeed, one can check that the function

\displaystyle h(\chi)=\chi\cdot{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},1,\frac{5}{4};\frac{4}{3},\frac{5}{3},2;\frac{256}{27}\cdot\chi\right),

(18)

satisfies the functional identity

\displaystyle\chi=\frac{1}{256}\left(1-\sqrt{1-8h(\chi)}\right)\left(3+\sqrt{1-8h(\chi)}\right)^{3}.

(19)

We therefore see that the hypergeometric (
11
) obtained earlier is, in fact, an algebraic function that can be defined as a root of (
19
).
1
1

1

Other examples of hypergeometric functions which can be expressed algebraically include those on Schwarz’s list
[
Schwarz1873
]
, which is summarized
on Wikipedia
.

3
Solution via dualization

The above result can also be derived in a different way. The details of this procedure do not depend on the sign of our constant
k
nor the signature, so we will set
k=1
and take Minkowski signature for concreteness. The undeformed Lagrangian (
2
) is then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=\frac{1}{k}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=-2F_{01}^{2},

(20)

which can be equivalently expressed by dualizing the field strength to a scalar, as in

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}+\phi\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}+2\phi F_{01}.

(21)

The equation of motion for
\phi
arising from (
21
) is

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}_{0}}{\delta\phi}=\phi+2F_{01}=0,

(22)

so
\phi=-2F_{01}
, and then replacing
\phi
with its equation of motion yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(-2F_{01}\right)^{2}+2\left(-2F_{01}\right)F_{01}=-2F_{01}^{2},

(23)

which matches (
20
). On the other hand, (
21
) is easy to
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
deform. After coupling to gravity, one has

\displaystyle S_{0}[g]=\left(\frac{1}{2}\int\sqrt{-g}\,\phi^{2}\,d^{2}x\right)+\left(\int\phi\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}\,d^{2}x\right),

(24)

where the second term is purely topological and thus independent of the metric. The undeformed Lagrangian, then, is a pure potential term
V(\phi)=\phi^{2}
for the boson
\phi
. The solution to the
T\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
flow equation at finite
\lambda
for a general potential is well-known
[
Cavaglia:2016oda
,
Bonelli:2018kik
]
; in this case, one finds

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}}{1-\frac{\lambda}{2}\phi^{2}}+\phi\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{2}}{2-\lambda\phi^{2}}+2\phi F_{01}.

(25)

We now integrate out
\phi
. The equation of motion for
\phi
arising from (
25
) is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}{\delta\phi}

\displaystyle=2\phi+F_{01}\left(2-\lambda\phi^{2}\right)^{2},

(26)

or

\displaystyle F_{01}=-\frac{2\phi}{(2-\lambda\phi^{2})^{2}}.

(27)

To proceed, we series expand (
27
) in
\phi
to find

\displaystyle F_{01}=-\frac{\phi}{2}-\frac{\lambda\phi^{3}}{2}-\frac{3\lambda^{2}\phi^{5}}{8}-\frac{\lambda^{3}\phi^{7}}{4}-\frac{5\lambda^{4}\phi^{9}}{32}+\mathcal{O}\left(\phi^{11}\right),

(28)

and then apply the Lagrange inversion theorem to find a series expansion for
\phi
in terms of
F_{01}
, yielding

\displaystyle\phi=-2F_{01}+8\lambda F_{01}^{2}-72\lambda^{2}F_{01}^{5}+832\lambda^{3}F_{01}^{7}-10880\lambda^{4}F_{01}^{9}+\mathcal{O}\left(F_{01}^{11}\right).

(29)

Substituting the expansion (
29
) into the action (
25
), and expressing the result in terms of
F^{2}=F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=-2F_{01}^{2}
, gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=F^{2}\left(1+\lambda F^{2}+3\lambda F^{4}+13\lambda^{3}F^{6}+68\lambda^{4}F^{8}+\cdots\right).

(30)

The Taylor coefficients appearing in (
30
) are precisely those of the hypergeometric (
10
). The procedure of iteratively solving (
27
) for
\phi
and substituting into (
25
), therefore, reproduces

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=F^{2}\cdot{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},1,\frac{5}{4};\frac{4}{3},\frac{5}{3},2;\frac{256}{27}\cdot\lambda F^{2}\right)

(31)

which matches the solution which we derived by different methods above.

This procedure – dualizing the field strength
F^{2}
to a scalar
\phi
,
{T\overline{T}}
deforming the scalar action, and then dualizing back – is closely related to an observation made in
[
Conti:2018jho
]
. There the authors noted that, although the deformed Lagrangian (
11
) is quite complicated, the corresponding Hamiltonian satisfies the simple relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\frac{\mathcal{H}_{0}}{1-\lambda\mathcal{H}_{0}},

(32)

where the Hamiltonian is a function of the conjugate momentum

\displaystyle\Pi^{1}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}}{\partial\dot{A}_{1}}.

(33)

The Legendre transform which converts the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
to the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
is mathematically equivalent to the process of dualizing the field strength
F_{01}
to a scalar
\phi
.

4
Comparison to Born-Infeld

For the moment, we specialize to the abelian case where the Born-Infeld action can be unambiguously defined. The Lagrangian (
11
) differs from the Born-Infeld action in two dimensions. To order
\lambda^{4}
, our solution has the series expansion

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=F^{2}+\lambda F^{4}+3\lambda^{2}F^{6}+13\lambda^{3}F^{8}+68\lambda^{4}F^{10}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5}).

(34)

On the other hand, the Born-Infeld action (after normalizing the coefficient of
F^{2}
to match (
34
) at order
F^{2}
) has the expansion

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\lambda}\sqrt{1+4\lambda F^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}+F^{2}-\lambda F^{4}+2\lambda^{2}F^{6}-5\lambda^{3}F^{8}+14\lambda^{4}F^{10}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5}).

(35)

Although the Taylor coefficients for the Born-Infeld action and the “hypergeometric action” differ, both exhibit a critical value for the electric field. In the case of Born-Infeld, this is obvious; replacing
F^{2}=-2F_{01}^{2}
, we see that the action

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\lambda}\sqrt{1-8\lambda F_{01}^{2}}

(36)

is only real for

\displaystyle F_{01}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{8\lambda}}.

(37)

To see the critical electric field for the action
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)
defined in (
10
), it is most convenient to use the implicit form (
19
):

\displaystyle\lambda F^{2}=\frac{1}{256}\left(1-\sqrt{1-8\lambda\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}\right)\left(3+\sqrt{1-8\lambda\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}\right)^{3}.

(38)

The right side is maximized when
\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{8\lambda}
, where it takes the value
\frac{27}{256}
, which means that

\displaystyle F^{2}<\frac{27}{256\lambda}.

(39)

Recall that our Lagrangian (
2
) contained an overall constant to track signs; to restore factors of
k
, we replace
F^{2}\to\frac{1}{k}F^{2}
. In Minkowski signature, we should take
k<0
so that
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{k}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{2}{k}F_{01}^{2}
is positive. Letting
k=-1
, we find

\displaystyle F_{01}<\sqrt{\frac{27}{512\lambda}},

(40)

which is a different critical value for the electric field than (
37
).

However, pure Yang-Mills theory in two dimensions has no propagating degrees of freedom, so the difference between the expansions (
34
) and (
35
) does not have much physical effect (at least in infinite volume). To detect the difference between these theories, we should couple the gauge field to matter, as we do in section
3
.

3
Non-Abelian Analogue of DBI

In this section, we will consider an action for a Yang-Mills gauge field
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
coupled to a scalar
\phi
in some representation of the gauge group. The undeformed Lagrangian is taken to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{\mu\nu}_{a}+|D\phi|^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv F^{2}+|D\phi|^{2},

(1)

where we again use the shorthand
F^{2}=\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right)
. We have set the overall constant
k
, which appears in (
2
), equal to
1
for simplicity. If one were to retain this constant, however, one would need an extra minus sign in (
1
) in Minkowski signature.

In what follows, we will also define
x=F^{2}
and
y=|D\phi|^{2}
for convenience; here

\displaystyle|D\phi|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(D_{\mu}\phi\right)\left(D^{\mu}\phi\right)^{\ast},

\displaystyle D_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}-iA_{\mu},

(2)

and gauge group indices will be suppressed.

At finite
\lambda
, we take a general ansatz of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=f(\lambda,x=F^{2},y=|D\phi|^{2}).

(3)

The stress tensor components
T_{\mu\nu}^{(\lambda)}
for the Lagrangian (
3
), and the differential equation arising from (
5
