can be determined from the known traces
\tr( M^i )
for
i=1,\cdots,d
.

The upshot of this theorem is that, in
d
spacetime dimensions, there are only
\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
independent trace structures
\mathrm{tr}\big(F^{k}\big)
for an antisymmetric
2
-tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
. The reason for the floor function is because of a slight difference between the cases of even and odd dimensions. For odd
d
, the determinant of any antisymmetric matrix vanishes, as does the trace of any odd power of the matrix. So in this case, if
d=2k+1
, the only independent trace structures are

\displaystyle\tr( F^2 )\,,\tr( F^4 )\,,\cdots\,,\tr F^{2k}\,.

(23)

Therefore there are
k=\frac{d-1}{2}
independent quantities. In even dimension
d=2k
, where the determinant of
F
may be non-zero, the independent trace structures are

\displaystyle\tr( F^2 )\,,\tr( F^4 )\,,\cdots\,,\tr F^{2k}\,.

(24)

so there are
k=\frac{d}{2}
independent quantities. Therefore, for the general case, we need
\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
trace structures in order to express an arbitrary scalar constructed from
F_{\mu\nu}
.

3
Deforming a General Lagrangian

In the previous subsection, we have seen that a general scalar quantity built from an antisymmetric
2
-form
F_{\mu\nu}
in
d
dimensions can be expressed as a function of the independent traces
\tr( F^2 )
,
\tr( F^4 )
,
\cdots
,
F^{2k}
where
k=\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
. In particular, the Lagrangian is a scalar and therefore takes this form, so the most general classical theory of an abelian gauge field can be written

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(F)=\mathcal{L}\left(\tr( F^2 ),\tr( F^4 ),\cdots,\tr( F^{2k} )\right)\,,

(25)

For convenience, we will define a set of variables
x_{i}
defined by

\displaystyle x_{i}=\tr( F^{2i} )\,,

(26)

for
i=1,\cdots,k
. We now wish to compute the stress tensor associated with the Lagrangian (
25
) so that we can construct the
{T\overline{T}}
deformation. Using our convention (
8
) for the Hilbert stress tensor and
g_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
in Euclidean signature, one has

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\cdot\frac{\delta x_{i}}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}\Bigg|_{g=\delta}\,.

(27)

Restoring metric factors, each
x_{i}
has the expansion

\displaystyle x_{i}=g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{0}\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\alpha_{0}}\,.

(28)

Computing the metric derivative thus yields
2i
separate terms, one for each factor of
g^{\alpha\beta}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta x_{i}}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\nu\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{0}\mu}F_{\nu\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\alpha_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad+\cdots+g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-2}\beta_{2i-2}}F_{\beta_{0}\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\mu}F_{\nu\alpha_{0}}\,.

(29)

Each of the terms appearing in (
3
) can be brought into an identical form via a re-labeling of indices. This must have been true on general grounds, since each term in (
3
) is obtained by removing one metric factor
g^{\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}}
and replacing the corresponding indices as
\alpha_{k}\to\mu
,
\beta_{k}\to\nu
. But since the entire expression
x_{i}
is invariant under cyclic permutations of
k
, each such term is identical and proportional to
F_{\mu\nu}^{2i}
.

However, one can also prove this claim explicitly. In each term, we simply push the factor of
F
with the
\nu
index all the way to the left, push the
F
with the
\mu
index to the right, and then cyclically permute the indices. For example, in the second term we have

\displaystyle g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{0}\mu}F_{\nu\alpha_{2}}F_{\beta_{2}\alpha_{3}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\alpha_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\nu\alpha_{2}}F_{\beta_{2}\alpha_{3}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\alpha_{0}}F_{\beta_{0}\mu}

\displaystyle\quad=g^{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-2}\beta_{2i-2}}F_{\nu\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-3}\alpha_{2i-2}}F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{0}}F_{\beta_{0}\mu}

\displaystyle\quad=g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-2}\beta_{2i-2}}g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\nu\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-3}\alpha_{2i-2}}F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\mu}\,.

(30)

In going from the first step to the second, we have simply commuted
F_{\beta_{0}\mu}
and
F_{\nu\alpha_{2}}
. To go to the third step, we relabeled each
\alpha^{k}
and
\beta^{k}
to
\alpha^{k-1}
and
\beta^{k-1}
for
k=2,\cdots,2i-1
. In the final step, we relabeled
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}
to
\alpha_{i-1},\beta_{i-1}
. The final expression is manifestly equivalent to the term on the first line of (
3
).

We note that this re-labeling procedure requires us to commute factors of
F_{\beta\alpha}
past one another. In the non-abelian context, to be investigated in Section
5
, the field strengths have non-trivial commutators so that this calculation is more subtle.

Using a similar cyclic manipulation in each of the
2i
terms to bring them all into the same form, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\delta x_{i}}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=2ig^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\nu\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\mu}\equiv 2iF^{2i}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(31)

where again we use the notation

\displaystyle F^{2i}_{\mu\nu}=g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{2i-1}\beta_{2i-1}}F_{\mu\alpha_{1}}F_{\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots F_{\beta_{2i-2}\alpha_{2i-1}}F_{\beta_{2i-1}\nu}\,.

(32)

Therefore, the stress tensor for a general Lagrangian is

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}-4\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}F^{2i}_{\mu\nu}\,.

(33)

Next we compute the appropriate bilinears. We have

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\left(\delta^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}-4\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}F^{2i,\mu\nu}\right)\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}-4\sum_{j=1}^{k}j\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{j}}F^{2j}_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{2}d-8\mathcal{L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\tr( F^{2i} )+16\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}ij\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{j}}F^{2i,\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}^{2j}\,.

(34)

Meanwhile, the trace is

\displaystyle\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}=\mathcal{L}d-4\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\tr( F^{2i} )\,,

(35)

and its square is

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\right)^{2}=\mathcal{L}^{2}d^{2}-8\mathcal{L}d\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\tr( F^{2i} )+16\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}ij\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{j}}\tr( F^{2i} )\tr( F^{2j} )\,.

(36)

As we argued in the introduction, in order to treat flow equations by a general linear combination of stress tensor bilinears, it is enough to consider flow equations of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=T^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}-r\left(\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\right)^{2}\,.

(37)

Deforming the Lagrangian by this linear combination gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}

\displaystyle=(d-rd^{2})\mathcal{L}^{2}-8\mathcal{L}(1-rd)\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}x_{i}+16\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}ij\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{j}}\left(x_{i+j}-rx_{i}x_{j}\right)\,.

(38)

Although (
38
) is exact, it is not expressed as a closed flow equation using only the set of independent trace structures
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}
. This is because term involving
x_{i+j}=\tr( F^{2(i+j)} )
introduces dependence on traces
\tr( F^{ 2 n} )
for integers
n>k
. These traces are not independent of the lower traces, as we have argued, and must therefore be re-expressed in terms of the other independent trace structures using the Cayley-Hamilton theorem.

It is unwieldy, though possible, to perform this procedure and write a closed flow equation using only the independent trace structures
x_{i}
by using equations (
21
), (
22
), and the definition (
18
) of the
c_{i}
. However, we will prefer to leave this implicit and only perform the necessary substitutions as needed in special cases later in this chapter.

Despite requiring the implicit definition of higher traces in terms of lower ones, (
38
) reveals some general structure about these
{T\overline{T}}
-like flows in arbitrary dimension, such as the dependence on
r
and
d
and the introduction of dependence on higher traces via the final term. For instance, one can see immediately that the flow equation simplifies for certain choices of
r
such as
r=\frac{1}{d}
, for which some of the terms on the right vanish. One can also see an interesting structure in the large
d
limit. If we retaining only the leading term on the right side as
d\to\infty
, the flow equation reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=-rd^{2}\mathcal{L}^{2}\,,

(39)

which has the simple solution

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{0}}{1+d^{2}\mathcal{L}_{0}r\lambda}\,.

(40)

For negative
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, such as
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{4}F^{2}
in Lorentzian signature, or for positive
\mathcal{L}_{0}
but negative
\lambda
or
r
, we see that (
40
) has a singularity at finite
\lambda
. This is reminiscent of the structure we saw in equation (
35
) of Chapter
3
, namely

\displaystyle V(\lambda,\phi)=\frac{V(0,\phi)}{1-\lambda V(0,\phi)}\,.

(41)

In both cases, it appears that the
{T\overline{T}}
deformation has led to a pole in the Lagrangian for certain field configurations. As we stressed in Chapter
3
, this is a purely classical result, and one cannot necessarily draw inferences about the structure of the quantum mechanical theory from this observation. However, this structure suggests the possibility of a modification of the vacuum structure in the theory – one might naively expect such an infinite potential barrier to cut off different sectors of the Hilbert space from one another. It would be interesting to study this possibility in greater detail, perhaps in a simplified quantum mechanical (i.e.
(0+1)
-dimensional) example.

Finally, we comment on one additional manipulation of (
38
) that may be useful in obtaining series solutions for certain flows. The Lagrangian may always be expanded as a power series in the variables
x_{k}=\tr( F^{2k} )
. We recall from footnote
2
that, in our conventions, the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}
has mass dimension
\frac{d}{2}
and thus
x_{k}
has mass dimension
kd
. Likewise,
\lambda
has length dimension
d
so combinations like
\lambda^{k}x_{k}
are dimensionless. We will now consider the combinations of terms that can appear in the power series expansion of the Lagrangian, subject to the usual requirement that each term have mass dimension
d
.

The coefficient of
x_{1}=F^{2}
, the free kinetic term, already has mass dimension
d
and thus appears with no powers of
\lambda
. Additional terms must be suppressed by powers of
\lambda
; for instance,
\lambda x_{2}
and
\lambda x_{1}^{2}
have the appropriate dimension to appear in the Lagrangian. More generally, a term with
2m
factors of
F_{\mu\nu}
must appear multiplying
\lambda^{m-1}
. The total mass dimension of a term
x_{1}^{n_{1}}x_{2}^{n_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{n_{k}}
is
\left(n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}\right)d
, and must therefore appear with a factor of
\lambda^{n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}-1}
. Thus the most general expansion of the Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\sum_{N}c_{N}\lambda^{n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}-1}x_{1}^{n_{1}}x_{2}^{n_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{n_{k}}\,.

(42)

Here
N
represents a multi-index of
k
non-negative integers
n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}
(not all zero). If we are beginning from an undeformed theory which is the ordinary Maxwell Lagrangian, the initial condition
\mathcal{L}(\lambda=0)=-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}
fixes the coefficient
N_{0}=\{1,0,0,\cdots,0\}
to be
c_{N_{0}}=-\frac{1}{4}
.

Such an expansion simplifies the problem of finding a series solution to our master equation (
38
), since now the task is reduced to determining each of the coefficients
c_{N}
. For instance, the left side of the flow equation is now

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=\sum_{N>N_{0}}c_{N}\left(n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}-1\right)\lambda^{n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}-2}x_{1}^{n_{1}}x_{2}^{n_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{n_{k}}\,,

(43)

where now the notation
\sum_{N>N_{0}}
means to sum over all multi-indices
N=\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}\}
except for
\{1,0,\cdots,0\}
. Likewise, each of the
x_{i}
derivatives appearing in the flow equation can be simply evaluated as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{j}}=\sum_{N\,,\,n_{j}>0}c_{N}\,n_{j}\,\lambda^{n_{1}+2n_{2}+\cdots+kn_{k}-1}x_{1}^{n_{1}}x_{2}^{n_{2}}\cdots x_{j}^{n_{j}-1}\cdots x_{k}^{n_{k}}\,,

(44)

where similarly
\sum_{N,n_{j}>0}
means to sum over all multi-indices
N=\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}\}
where
n_{j}
is at least
1
.

After eliminating all of the higher trace structures
x_{n}
for
n>k
in the master flow equation (
38
) using Cayley-Hamilton, one can then substitute (
43
) on the left and (
44
) for each
x_{i}
derivative on the right. The result, though perhaps complicated, is in principle a perturbative expansion that allows us to solve for the coefficients
c_{N}
order-by-order in
\lambda
.

4
Low-Dimensional Examples

To illustrate the general formalism developed in the preceding subsection, here we will develop the flow equations in
d=2
and
d=3
. These cases are especially simple since there is only one independent trace structure
x_{1}
, so we need only express
x_{2}
in terms of
x_{1}
using the Cayley-Hamilton theorem. Since we have studied these examples before, this will also provide a usesful check of whether the formalism recovers previous results.

Two Dimensions

In two dimensions, the Cayley-Hamilton theorem reads

\displaystyle M^{2}-(\tr( M ))M+\det( M )I_{2}=0\,.

(45)

For an antisymmetric matrix
M=F
, then, we have
\tr( F )=0
and
F^{2}=-\det( F )I_{2}
. Taking traces, one has

\displaystyle\tr( F^2 )=-2\det( F )\,.

(46)

If we first multiply by
F^{2}
and then take traces, we instead find

\displaystyle\tr( F^4 )=-\det( F )\tr( F^2 )=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tr( F )\right)^{2}\,.

(47)

In terms of our variables
x_{i}=\tr( F^{2i} )
, this means that

\displaystyle x_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{2}\,.

(48)

Using this, our general flow equation (
38
) with
d=2
becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=2\left(1-2r\right)\mathcal{L}^{2}-8\mathcal{L}(1-2r)x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}+8\left(1-2r\right)x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(49)

This should match the result that we derived in Chapter
5
. Indeed, if
r=1
the flow equation reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=-2\mathcal{L}^{2}+8\mathcal{L}x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}-8x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(50)

We may now multiply both sides by
-\frac{1}{2}
and absorb this factor into a re-scaling of
\lambda
on the left side, which gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{2}-4\mathcal{L}x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}+4x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(51)

This matches (
10
), as expected. We note that we have re-scaled
\lambda
by a negative constant, which reverses the sign; this corresponds to the fact that our convention for the “good sign” of
\lambda
is opposite that in Chapter
5
. We will see a similar re-scaling by a constant
a=-8
when comparing to Born-Infeld in the next section.

Three Dimensions

In the case of
d=3
, the Cayley-Hamilton result (
17
) gives the identity

\displaystyle M^{3}-\left(\tr( M )\right)M^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\tr( M )\right)^{2}-\tr( M^2 )\right)M-\det( M )I_{3}=0\,.

(52)

Let
M=F
be antisymmetric. In odd dimensions, the determinant of any antisymmetric matrix vanishes, and we manifestly also have
\tr( M )=0
, so the condition becomes

\displaystyle F^{3}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)F=0\,.

(53)

We may multiply through by
F
and take the trace to find

\displaystyle\tr( F^4 )=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{2}\,,

(54)

which gives the same condition
x_{2}=\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{2}
which we found in
d=2
. The flow equation, however, will differ because it explicitly depends on
d
. The master equation (
38
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=3(1-3r)\mathcal{L}^{2}-8\mathcal{L}(1-3r)x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}+8(1-2r)x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(55)

This matches the result (
1
) which we derived earlier.

3
Comparison to Born-Infeld

In this section, we will examine the flow equation for gauge theories in general dimension to see whether any such flow can yield the Born-Infeld action from the Maxwell Lagrangian as the seed theory, besides the known case in
d=4
.

1
Perturbative Comparison in
\lambda

It will be sufficient to check whether Born-Infeld-type actions can satisfy a
{T\overline{T}}
-like flow equation to second order in
\lambda
. To make this comparison, we first expand the Born-Infeld Lagrangian to the first few orders. First note that, since
\det( M )=\det( M^T )
for any matrix
M
, we have

\displaystyle\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}+kF_{\mu\nu}\right)=\det\left(\delta_{\nu\mu}+kF_{\nu\mu}\right)=\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-kF_{\mu\nu}\right)\,.

(1)

Therefore, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\sqrt{\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}+kF_{\mu\nu}\right)}

\displaystyle=\Big[\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}+kF_{\mu\nu}\right)\det\left(\delta_{\alpha\beta}-kF_{\alpha\beta}\right)\Big]^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\left[\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-k^{2}F^{2}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\,.

(2)

On the other hand, one has
\det( A )=\exp\left(\tr( \log( A ) )\right)
for any matrix
A
, so

\displaystyle\left(\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-k^{2}F^{2}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\tr\left(\log\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-k^{2}F^{2}_{\mu\nu}\right)\right)\right)\,.

(3)

In order to compare the Born-Infeld action to
{T\overline{T}}
-flows up to order
\lambda^{2}
, we must Taylor-expand (
3
) to order
k^{6}
. This gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}+kF_{\mu\nu}\right)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{k^{2}}{4}\tr( F^2 )+\frac{k^{4}}{4}\left(\frac{1}{8}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^4 )\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{k^{6}}{6}\left(\frac{1}{64}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{3}-\frac{3}{16}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^4 )+\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^6 )\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{8}\right)\,.

(4)

Following the conventions in footnote
2
, where the gauge coupling
g
has been absorbed into the field strength
F
, the combination
\sqrt{\lambda}F_{\mu\nu}
is dimensionless. Thus the appropriate form of a Born-Infeld type Lagrangian which could potentially arise from a
{T\overline{T}}
flow is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{a\lambda}\left(-1+\sqrt{\det\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}+\sqrt{a\lambda}F_{\mu\nu}\right)}\right),

(5)

where
a
is arbitrary and the coefficient
\sqrt{a\lambda}
in front of
F_{\mu\nu}
is chosen so that the Lagrangian reduces to
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{4}\tr( F^2 )=\frac{1}{4}F^{2}
as
\lambda\to 0
. Using (
1
) with
k=\sqrt{a\lambda}
, then, the expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
to order
\lambda^{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\tr( F^2 )+\frac{a\lambda}{4}\left(\frac{1}{8}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^4 )\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{a^{2}\lambda^{2}}{6}\left(\frac{1}{64}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{3}-\frac{3}{16}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^4 )+\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^6 )\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{3}\right)\,.

(6)

Let’s now compare this to the Lagrangian one would get using the master flow equation (
38
). Beginning with the initial condition
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(d-rd^{2})x_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{2}(1-rd)+\left(x_{2}-rx_{1}^{2}\right)\,

\displaystyle=x_{2}+x_{1}^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d}{16}-\left(1-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{16}\right)r\right)\,.

(7)

Note that, when
d=4
, the quantity multiplying
r
in the parentheses of (
1
) vanishes, and the coefficient of
x_{1}^{2}
simply becomes
-\frac{1}{4}
. Assuming that
d\neq 4
, in order for the coefficients to match the order
\lambda
expansion of Born-Infeld we need

\displaystyle a=-8\,,\qquad r=\frac{1}{d-4}\,.

(8)

This scaling by
a=-8
agrees with the factor of
\frac{1}{8}
appearing in (
12
). The overall sign difference arises from the fact that our convention for the “good sign” of
\lambda
in this chapter is the opposite of that which we have previously used in two dimensions, for instance in Chapter
5
. One can see this by comparing equation (
37
) to equation (
5
). For the present purposes, it is sufficient to assume
\lambda<0
so that
a\lambda
is positive.

Thus, to order
\lambda
, the deformed Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}+\lambda x_{2}-\frac{\lambda}{4}x_{1}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\,.

(9)

Repeating the process by plugging this deformed Lagrangian into the master equation and extracting the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
piece, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{-12+d-32r+12rd-rd^{2}}{8}\,x_{1}^{3}+\frac{20-d+32r-12rd+rd^{2}}{2}\,x_{1}x_{2}-16x_{3}\right)\,.

(10)

Substituting the required value of
r=\frac{1}{d-4}
, this becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=\lambda^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}^{3}+3x_{1}x_{2}-8x_{3}\right)\,.

(11)

The coefficients of
x_{1}^{3},x_{1}x_{2},x_{3}
in this term are
-\frac{1}{4},3,
and
-8
, respectively, which do not match the coefficients in (
1
) when
a=-8
, which are
-\frac{1}{6}
,
2
, and
-\frac{16}{3}
. Thus the Born-Infeld action can never be obtained by a flow involving stress tensor bilinears in any number of dimensions where
\tr( F^6 )
is independent of lower traces, i.e.
d\geq 6
.

In
d<6
dimensions,
x_{3}
can be expressed in terms of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
so checking the coefficients at order
\lambda^{2}
requires eliminating
x_{3}
using the Cayley-Hamilton theorem. We have already checked most of the cases
d<6
; in particular,
d=2
gives the hypergeometric action discussed in Chapter
5
, we saw that
d=3
does not give Born-Infeld in Section
1
, and we know from Chapter
4
that
{T\overline{T}}
in four dimensions
does
generate a Born-Infeld type action. The only remaining case to check (besides
d=1
, which is trivial) is
d=5
spacetime dimensions, where
\tr( F^2 )
and
\tr( F^4 )
are the independent trace structures. We perform this explicit check in the next subsection.

2
Deformation in
d=5

We will now handle one edge case which is not included in the preceding analysis, namely
d=5
. In this case, the expression (
10
) for the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
term in the
{T\overline{T}}
flow is formally correct. However, since the trace
x_{3}=\tr( F^6 )
is not independent of the lower
x_{i}
in five dimensions, we must eliminate it in favor of lower traces using the Cayley-Hamilton theorem. In five dimensions, (
17
) reads

\displaystyle M^{5}-(\tr( M ))M^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\tr( M )\right)^{2}-\tr( M^2 )\right)M^{3}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\left(\left(\tr( M )\right)^{3}-3\tr( M^2 )\tr( M )+2\tr( M^3 )\right)M^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{24}\left(\tr( M )^{4}+8\tr(M^3)\tr( M )+3\left(\tr( M^2 )\right)^{2}-6\tr( M^2 )\left(\tr( M )\right)^{2}-6\tr(M^4)\right)M

\displaystyle\quad-\det( M )I_{5}=0\,.

(12)

Letting
M=F
be antisymmetric, which implies that
\tr( F )=\tr( F^3 )=\det( F )=0
, this becomes

\displaystyle F^{5}-\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^2 )F^{3}+\frac{1}{24}\left(3\left(\tr\big( F^2 \big)\right)^{2}-6\tr\big( F^4 \big)\right)F=0\,.

(13)

Multiplying both sides of (
13
) by
F
and tracing, one finds

\displaystyle\tr( F^6 )=\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^4 )-\frac{1}{24}\left(3\left(\tr\big( F^2 \big)\right)^{2}-6\tr\big( F^4 \big)\right)\tr( F^2 )\,,

(14)

or in terms of the variables
x_{i}
,

\displaystyle x_{3}=\frac{3}{4}x_{1}x_{2}-\frac{1}{8}x_{1}^{3}\,.

(15)

This means that the order
\lambda^{2}
term arising from the
{T\overline{T}}
flow is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=\frac{3\lambda^{2}}{4}\left(x_{1}^{3}-4x_{1}x_{2}\right)\,.

(16)

We can compare this to the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
term in the expansion (
1
) of Born-Infeld, after substituting the constraint (
15
), which yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(x_{1}^{3}-4x_{1}x_{2}\right)\,.

(17)

Since (
16
) and (
17
) differ by an overall multiplicative factor, we conclude that the Born-Infeld action is not generated by a
{T\overline{T}}
flow in five dimensions. This completes the proof that the only dimension in which the Born-Infeld action arises from a
{T\overline{T}}
-like deformation of the free Maxwell Lagrangian, for any choice of
r
, is in
d=4
.

3
Deformation in
d=4

Although we have already seen in Chapter
4
that
{T\overline{T}}
deformation of the
4
-dimensional Maxwell action yields the Born-Infeld action, it is a useful check of our general formalism to see how this emerges from the master flow equation (
38
). In particular, since we have shown that this flow equation does
not
yield Born-Infeld in any
d>4
, it is instructive to see how the numerical factors appearing in the Cayley-Hamilton theorem conspire to give a deformed action of square-root type in
d=4
.

As we mentioned around equation (
14
), in four spacetime dimensions the two independent scalars that can be constructed from
F_{\mu\nu}
are

\displaystyle x_{1}=F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\mu}=\tr( F^2 )\,,\qquad x_{2}=F^{\mu\sigma}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}F_{\rho\mu}=\tr( F^4 )\,.

(18)

However, our master flow equation (
38
) will introduce dependence on two additional trace structures,
x_{3}=\tr( F^{6} )
and
x_{4}=\tr( F^{8} )
. First we must express these in terms of lower trace structures. For a general
4\times 4
matrix
M
, the Cayley-Hamilton theorem (
17
) reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=M^{4}-\left(\tr( M )\right)M^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\tr( M )\right)^{2}-\tr( M^2 )\right)M^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{6}\left(\left(\tr( M )\right)^{3}-3\tr( M^2 )\tr( M )+2\tr( M^3 )\right)M+\det( M )I_{4}\,.

(19)

If we specialize to the case where
M=F
, which is antisymmetric and thus has vanishing trace (as does
F^{3}
), we have

\displaystyle 0=F^{4}-\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^2 )F^{2}+\det( F )I_{4}\,.

(20)

By taking traces, we can solve for the determinant as

\displaystyle\det( F )=\frac{1}{8}\left(\tr\big( F^2 \big)\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\tr( F^4 )\,.

(21)

Similarly, if we first multiply (
20
) by
F^{2}
and then take traces, we find

\displaystyle\tr( F^6 )=\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^4 )-\det( F )\tr( F^2 )\,,

(22)

and if we first multiply (
20
) by
F^{4}
and then take traces, one obtains

\displaystyle\tr( F^8 )=\frac{1}{2}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^6 )-\det( F )\tr( F^4 )\,.

(23)

Expressed in terms of
x_{i}=\tr( F^{2i} )
, these equations can be solved to find

\displaystyle x_{3}=-\frac{1}{8}x_{1}\left(x_{1}^{2}-6x_{2}\right)\,,\qquad x_{4}=-\frac{1}{16}\left(x_{1}^{4}-4x_{1}^{2}x_{2}-4x_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

(24)

The numerical factors appearing in (
24
) work out such that the general argument that a
{T\overline{T}}
flow disagrees with Born-Infeld at order
\lambda^{2}
, presented in Section
1
for
d\geq 6
, fails in four dimensions. This was already suggested by the term at leading order in
\lambda
, where the coefficients automatically agreed in
d=4
and required
r=\frac{1}{d-4}
otherwise. However, we can also check that the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
piece works. Substituting in
d=4
in equation (
10
), one finds

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{T\overline{T}}}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(x_{1}^{3}-8x_{1}x_{2}+16x_{3}\right)\,,

(25)

and using our expression (
24
) for
x_{3}
, this is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{T\overline{T}}}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(x_{1}^{3}-4x_{1}x_{2}\right)\,,

(26)

On the other hand, when
a=-8
, the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
term in (
1
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{BI}\Big|_{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{64\lambda^{2}}{6}\left(\frac{1}{64}\left(\tr( F^2 )\right)^{3}-\frac{3}{16}\tr( F^2 )\tr( F^4 )+\frac{1}{2}\Tr( F^6 )\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(x_{1}^{3}-4x_{1}x_{2}\right)\,.

(27)

So in this case, there is an exact match at order
\lambda^{2}
. In fact, using the expressions (
24
), we can see how the all-orders flow equation emerges, which has a Born-Infeld solution as we saw in Chapter
4
. Beginning from the master flow equation (
38
) and letting
d=4
,
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(\lambda,x_{1},x_{2})
, then eliminating
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(4-16r\right)\mathcal{L}^{2}-8\left(1-4r\right)\mathcal{L}\left(x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}+2x_{2}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{2}}\right)+16\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\left(x_{2}-rx_{1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+16\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{2}}x_{1}\left(x_{1}^{2}-6x_{2}+8rx_{2}\right)+\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{2}}\right)^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}^{4}+x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+(1-4r)x_{2}^{2}\right)\right)\,.

(28)

Although this differential equation is quite complicated, it simplifies if we make the ansatz

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2\alpha\lambda}\left(-1+\sqrt{1-\alpha\lambda x_{1}+\lambda^{2}\left(\beta x_{2}+\gamma x_{1}^{2}\right)}\right)\,.

(29)

With this ansatz, the differential equation (
3
) reduces to a set of algebraic consistency conditions for the parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,r
, whose solution is

\displaystyle\alpha=-4\,,\quad\beta=-16\,,\quad\gamma=8\,,\quad r=\frac{1}{2}\,.

(30)

Therefore, the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{8\lambda}\left(-1+\sqrt{1+4\lambda x_{1}+\lambda^{2}\left(8x_{1}^{2}-16x_{2}\right)}\right)\,

(31)

is an exact solution to the flow equation (
38
) in
d=4
, as expected from the analysis of Chapter
4
. This confirms that our formalism reproduces the known result in this case.

4
Born-Infeld-Type Seed Theories

One advantage of the general formalism of Section
2
is that it allows us to more uniformly treat
{T\overline{T}}
-flows with different initial conditions across various dimensions. As an application, we will be motivated by the deformation of a seed theory which is not the free Maxwell action, but rather the Born-Infeld action.

We will focus on the low-dimensional examples
d=2,3
, since here there is only a single trace structure
x_{1}
to worry about. In these cases, the seed theory which we wish to deform depends on
x_{1}
via the functional form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\left(\sqrt{1+c\alpha^{\prime}x_{1}}\right)\,.

(1)

where
\alpha^{\prime}
is dimensionful and
c
is a numerical constant. Deforming such a theory is more difficult than deforming the free Maxwell theory
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{4}x_{1}
, since in the latter case, the only dimensionful quantities in the problem are
\lambda
and
x_{1}
. We exploited this simplicity in previous analyses by making an ansatz of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x_{1},\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}f(\lambda x_{1})\,,

(2)

which allowed us to convert the
{T\overline{T}}
flow equation into an ordinary differential equation in the dimensionless variable
\lambda x_{1}
.

This strategy is not available when deforming a seed theory of the type (
1
), since now there are three dimensionful quantities
x_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\lambda
. This means that an ansatz of the form (
2
) is not sufficient. In two dimensions (where
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\lambda
have the same dimension, according to our conventions), we would need a more complicated ansatz such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}f\left(\lambda x_{1},\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\lambda}\right)\,,

(3)

which leads to a partial differential equation rather than an ordinary differential equation.

Although the promotion from an ODE to a PDE complicates the analysis, this complication is not special to a seed theory of the form (
1
). The additional difficulty merely comes from the presence of a second dimensionful constant
\alpha^{\prime}
in the undeformed theory. For this reason, although we are motivated by the deformation of Born-Infeld theories like (
5
) (which take the form (
1
) in
d=2
and
d=3
), we can in fact analyze flow equations beginning from a general seed theory of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},x_{1})\,.

(4)

By making an ansatz of the form (
3
), we can treat the deformation of all such seed theories simultaneously. The solution to this general problem reduces to the solution of the flow equation for a Born-Infeld-type seed theory in the special case (
1
).

1
Two Dimensions

As a first example, we will study this problem in two spacetime dimensions. Here there is a preferred value
r=1
for the relative coefficient in our deformation, which defines the usual two-dimensional
{T\overline{T}}
operator; for simplicity, we will take this value
r=1
in this subsection. As we saw in (
50
), in this case our master formula (
38
) with
\mathcal{L}=f(\lambda,x_{1})
reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{df}{d\lambda}=-2f^{2}+8fx_{1}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}-8x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(5)

In order to match the conventions of Chapter
5
, we will multiply both sides by
-\frac{1}{2}
and absorb this factor into a re-scaling of
\lambda
on the left side as in (
51
), so that the flow equation becomes

\displaystyle\frac{df}{d\lambda}=f^{2}-4fx_{1}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}+4x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(6)

As we described above, rather than the initial condition
f(\lambda=0)=\frac{1}{4}x_{1}
, we are motivated by a class of initial conditions includes the seed theory

\displaystyle f(\lambda=0)=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\sqrt{1+\alpha^{\prime}F^{2}}-1\right)\,.

(7)

On dimensional grounds, the solution to the flow equation beginning from an initial condition of this form can be expressed as

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}h\left(\lambda x_{1},\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\lambda}\right)\,.

(8)

In the limit as the ratio
\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\lambda}
goes to
0
, the undeformed theory reduces to the free Maxwell action, and the deformed theory is then the usual hypergeometric action. Changing variables to
\xi=\lambda x_{1},\eta=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}
, the differential equation becomes

\displaystyle h^{2}+\eta\frac{\partial h}{\partial\eta}+\left(1-4\xi\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}\right)h-\xi\left(1-4\xi\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}\right)\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}=0\,.

(9)

It is again convenient to switch to
\log
variables of the form

\displaystyle p=\log( \xi)\,,\qquad q=\log( \eta)\,,

(10)

and write
h(\xi,\eta)=w(p,q)
, so that

\displaystyle w^{2}+w+\frac{\partial w}{\partial q}+4\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial q}\right)^{2}-\left(1+4w\right)\frac{\partial w}{\partial p}=0\,.

(11)

Perhaps surprisingly, this equation is of the same form as (
15
) in Chapter
5
, which arose for a gauge field coupled to matter. In fact, (
11
) can be solved using the complete integral from
[
polyanin
]
as was used to solve (
15
).

We will review this solution technique here for convenience. Any partial differential equation which can be written as

\displaystyle 0=f_{1}(w)\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+f_{2}(w)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}+f_{3}(w)\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\right)^{2}+g_{1}(w)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}+g_{2}(w)\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}+h(w),

(12)

has a solution
w(x,y)
is given implicitly via

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=C_{1}x+C_{2}y+\int\frac{2F(w)\,dw}{G(w)\pm\sqrt{G(w)^{2}-4F(w)h(w)}},

\displaystyle F(w)

\displaystyle=C_{1}^{2}f_{1}(w)+C_{1}C_{2}f_{2}(w)+C_{2}^{2}f_{3}(w),

\displaystyle G(w)

\displaystyle=C_{1}g_{1}(w)+C_{2}g_{2}(w).

(13)

To match (
11
) onto the form (
12
), we identify the independent variables as
p\to x
,
q\to y
, and we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f_{1}=f_{2}=0\,,\quad f_{3}=4\,,\quad g_{1}=-(1+4w)\,,\\
&g_{2}=1\quad,\quad h(w)=w^{2}+w.\end{split}

(14)

Therefore, the functions
F
and
G
are

\displaystyle F(w)

\displaystyle=4C_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle G(w)

\displaystyle=C_{2}-C_{1}\left(1+4w\right)\,.

(15)

The solution via complete integral can hence be written as

\displaystyle C_{3}=C_{1}p+C_{2}q+\int\frac{8C_{2}^{2}\,dw}{C_{2}-C_{1}(1+4w)\pm\sqrt{\left(C_{2}-C_{1}(1+4w)\right)^{2}-16C_{2}^{2}\left(w^{2}+w\right)}}\,.

(16)

As with the corresponding complete integral from Chapter
5
, the integral on the right side of (
16
) can be evaluated in terms of square roots and inverse hyperbolic tangents (or equivalently logarithms), although the explicit expression may not be especially illuminating. The integration constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
are then determined by the initial conditions; for instance, in the case of a Born-Infeld seed theory one would impose the initial condition (
7
) and that the implicit solution reduce to (
19
) when
\eta=0
.

We will also record a particular special solution to the flow equation, parameterized by constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
which are not the same as the capital constants
C_{1},C_{2}
above. This is a special case of (
16
) where the integral simplifies. Restoring
\xi,\eta
, the implicit expression

\displaystyle(1+c_{1})\log( \xi)+\log( \eta)+c_{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}\log\left(\sqrt{c_{1}^{2}-8(c_{1}+1)(c_{1}+2)h(\xi,\eta)}-c_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+(4+3c_{1})\log\left(4+3c_{1}+\sqrt{c_{1}^{2}-8(1+c_{1})(2+c_{1})h(\xi,\eta)}\right)\,,

(17)

defines a function
h(\xi,\eta)
which is a particular solution to this flow equation. The form of the square roots inside the logarithms of (
1
) is reminiscent of those in Chapter
5
.

2
Three Dimensions

For completeness, we will also study the corresponding problem in three dimensions, although it is similar to the two-dimensional case but with different numerical factors. We saw in (
55
) that the master flow equation in three spacetime dimensions becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}=3(1-3r)\mathcal{L}^{2}-8\mathcal{L}(1-3r)x_{1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}+8(1-2r)x_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{2}\,.

(18)

As before, we will make an ansatz of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=f(x_{1},\beta,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}h\left(\lambda x_{1},\frac{\beta}{\lambda}\right)\,.

(19)

We now use the symbol
\beta
rather than
\alpha^{\prime}
to avoid confusion since
\alpha^{\prime}
is typically taken to have length dimension
2
in any spacetime dimension
d
(as it is the square of the string length), whereas in our conventions,
\lambda
has length dimension
d
so
\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\lambda}
is not dimensionless in
d=3
. Thus in this case, one can think of
\beta=\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{3/2}
and use this to form a dimensionless ratio
\frac{\beta}{\lambda}
.

Making the same change of variables

\displaystyle\xi=\lambda x_{1}\,,\qquad\eta=\frac{\beta}{\lambda}\,,

(20)

as in two dimensions, we arrive at the differential equation

\displaystyle\left(9r-3\right)h^{2}+h\left(-1+8(1-3r)\xi\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}\right)+\xi\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}\left(1+8(2r-1)\xi\frac{\partial h}{\partial\xi}\right)-\eta\frac{\partial h}{\partial\eta}=0\,.

(21)

We can once again switch to log variables
p=\log( \xi),q=\log( \eta)
to find

\displaystyle\left(9r-3\right)w^{2}-w+\frac{\partial w}{\partial p}+8(2r-1)\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial q}\right)^{2}+(8-24r)w\frac{\partial w}{\partial p}-\frac{\partial w}{\partial q}=0\,.

(22)

This PDE is still of the general form (
12
), so it may be solved with the same method of writing the solution implicitly using a complete integral. However, we will not belabor the point further by writing the corresponding formulas.

This concludes our discussion of deformations of more general seed theories, including those of Born-Infeld type, in
d=2
and
d=3
. The question of the corresponding deformations in
d\geq 4
is more interesting. In that case, since there are more trace structures involved, one would need to begin with an ansatz of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{\lambda}h\left(\lambda x_{1},\lambda^{2}x_{2}\,,\cdots,\lambda^{k}x_{k},\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\lambda}\right)\,,

(23)

which produces a partial differential equation in
k+1
variables. Barring some miraculous simplification, the complete integral techniques described here will not work in such cases, since this method applies only to partial differential equations in two variables.

5
Deformations of Non-Abelian Gauge Theories

In this section, we turn to the deformation of non-abelian gauge theories. We will have much less to say in this context, instead limiting ourselves to a few general observations and pointing out the complications which make this case more difficult.

We now assume that the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}T_{a}
carries an additional gauge index, where we will always use Greek letters for spacetime indices and Latin letters for gauge indices. We will assume that the Killing-Cartan form has been diagonalized and the generators
T^{a}
of our Lie algebra are scaled so that
\Tr( T^a T^b )=\delta^{ab}
, and write

\displaystyle F^{2}=\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right)=\sum_{a}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{\mu\nu a}\,,

(1)

where the capital trace
\Tr
refers to the trace over gauge indices.

The presence of additional indices means that we can build more inequivalent combinations of field strengths than in the abelian setting. For instance, in abelian gauge theories the two scalars involving four field strengths are

\displaystyle x_{1}^{2}=\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right)^{2}\,,\qquad x_{2}=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}F^{\rho\sigma}F_{\sigma\nu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\ma (2)

However, in a non-abelian theory, we can construct at least
6
inequivalent combinations:

\displaystyle x_{1,1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right)\right)^{2}=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{\mu\nu}_{a}F_{\alpha\beta}^{b}F^{\alpha\beta}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle x_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)\Tr\left(F^{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}\right)=F^{\mu\nu}_{a}F_{\mu\nu}^{b}F_{\alpha\beta}^{a}F^{\alpha\beta}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle x_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)=F_{\mu\nu a}F^{\mu\nu}_{b}F_{\alpha\beta c}F^{\alpha\beta}_{d}\Tr\left(T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}T^{d}\right)

\displaystyle x_{2,3}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}F^{\rho\sigma}\right)\Tr\left(F_{\sigma\nu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4. \displaystyle x_{2,5}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}F^{\rho\sigma}F_{\sigma\nu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\right)=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}{a}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptsc
